# Madrid

**Madrid**[^cite_note-11] is the capital and [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranked_lists_of_Spanish_municipalities#By_population) of Spain. It had a population of over 3.4 million in the city proper in 2025,[^cite_note-population-2] and a [metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_metropolitan_area) population of approximately 6.8 million.[^cite_note-pop-12] Madrid is the [second-largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Largest_cities_of_the_European_Union_by_population_within_city_limits) in the [European Union](9317.md) (EU), after [Berlin](3354.md),[^cite_note-13] and its [metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_metropolitan_area) is the [second-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_in_Europe_by_population) in the EU, after [Paris](22989.md).[^cite_note-demographia-3][^cite_note-citypopulation.de-14][^cite_note-un.org-15] The municipality covers an area of 605.77 square kilometres (233.89 sq mi).[^cite_note-area-1] Madrid lies on the [River Manzanares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manzanares_\(river\)) in the central part of the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) at about 660 metres (2,170 ft) above mean sea level. The capital city of both Spain and the surrounding [autonomous community of Madrid](69802.md),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoreno-Fernández202044-16] it is the political, economic and cultural centre of the country.[^cite_note-17]&#x20;

The primitive core of Madrid, a walled military outpost, dates back to the late 9th century, under the [Emirate of Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_C%C3%B3rdoba). Conquered by Christians in 1083 or 1085, it consolidated in the Late Middle Ages as a sizeable town of the [Crown of Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_Castile).[^cite_note-18] The development of Madrid as an administrative centre was fostered after 1561, as it became the permanent seat of the court of the [Hispanic Monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_Monarchy_\(political_entity\)). The following centuries were characterised by the reinforcement of Madrid's status within the framework of a centralised form of state-building.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEValenzuela_Rubio199968-19]&#x20;

The Madrid urban agglomeration has the [second-largest GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_by_GDP) in the [European Union](9317.md).[^cite_note-20] Madrid is ranked as an [alpha world city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpha_world_city) by the [Globalization and World Cities Research Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization_and_World_Cities_Research_Network).[^cite_note-GaWC-21] The metropolitan area hosts major Spanish companies such as [Telefónica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telef%C3%B3nica), [Iberia](98613.md), [BBVA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBVA) and [FCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fomento_de_Construcciones_y_Contratas).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoreno-Fernández202045-22] It concentrates the bulk of banking operations in Spain and it is the Spanish-speaking city generating the largest number of webpages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoreno-Fernández202045-22] Madrid houses the headquarters of [UN Tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Tourism), the [Ibero-American General Secretariat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibero-American_General_Secretariat) (SEGIB), the [Organization of Ibero-American States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Ibero-American_States) (OEI), and the [Public Interest Oversight Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Interest_Oversight_Board) (PIOB). Pursuant to the standardising role of the [Royal Spanish Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Spanish_Academy), Madrid is a centre for Spanish [linguistic prescriptivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linguistic_prescription).[^cite_note-23] Madrid organises fairs such as FITUR,[^cite_note-FITUR-24] ARCO,[^cite_note-ARCO-25] [SIMO TCI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SIMO_TCI)[^cite_note-26] and the [Madrid Fashion Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Fashion_Week).[^cite_note-27] Madrid is home to [football](10568.md) clubs [Real Madrid](26413.md) and [Atlético Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atl%C3%A9tico_Madrid).&#x20;

Madrid is one of Spain's leading destinations for [international tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_tourism). In 2023, it ranked second among Spanish tourist destinations with 5,757,815 international visitors.[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29] Madrid's main international source markets include the United States, Italy, France, the United Kingdom and Mexico.[^cite_note-30] Its landmarks include the [Plaza Mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_Mayor,_Madrid); the [Royal Palace of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Palace_of_Madrid); the [Royal Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Real) with its restored 1850 Opera House; the [Buen Retiro Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buen_Retiro_Park), founded in 1631; the 19th-century [National Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblioteca_Nacional_de_Espa%C3%B1a) building containing some of Spain's historical archives; many national museums;[^cite_note-31] and the [UNESCO World heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_world_heritage) "[Golden Triangle of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Triangle_of_Art)", located along the [Paseo del Prado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paseo_del_Prado) and comprising three art museums: the [Prado Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_del_Prado); the [Reina Sofía Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Nacional_Centro_de_Arte_Reina_Sof%C3%ADa), a museum of [modern art](163543.md); and the [Thyssen-Bornemisza Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thyssen-Bornemisza_Museum), which complements the holdings of the other two museums.[^cite_note-Overview_of_Madrid-32] The mayor is [José Luis Martínez-Almeida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Luis_Mart%C3%ADnez-Almeida) from the [People's Party](404727.md).[^cite_note-33]&#x20;

## Etymology

The origin of the name is unknown. There are various theories regarding the origin of the toponym "Madrid", all of them with problems when it comes to fully explaining the phonetic evolution of the toponym.[^cite_note-Orozco-34] The Latin theory (Madrid < *Matrice* < *Matrix*) has gained support in the past decades:&#x20;

- A [Celtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Celtic_languages) origin (Madrid < \**Magetoritum*;

  [^cite_note-Orozco-34]

  : 701&#x20;

  &#x20;with the root "-ritu" meaning "[ford](30432825.md)").

- From the Arabic *maǧrà* / majrā (meaning "water stream")

  [^cite_note-Orozco-34]

  : 701&#x20;

  &#x20;or [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): ‏[مجريط](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%D9%85%D8%AC%D8%B1%D9%8A%D8%B7#Arabic)‎, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *majrīṭ*, [lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation) '"spring", "fountain"'.

  [^cite_note-Khayat-35]

- A [Mozarabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andalusi_Romance) variant of the Latin *[matrix](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/matrix#Latin)*, *matricis* (also meaning "water stream").

  [^cite_note-Orozco-34]

  : 701&#x20;

Nicknames for Madrid include the plural *Los Madriles*[^cite_note-CVC-36] and *La Villa y Corte* (lit. 'the town and [court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_court)').&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Madrid)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Madrid).

The site of modern-day Madrid has been occupied since prehistoric times.[^cite_note-autogenerated4-37][^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] There are archaeological remains of the [Celtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celts) [Carpetani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpetani) settlement, [Roman villas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_villa),[^cite_note-40] a [Visigoth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visigoth) basilica near the church of Santa María de la Almudena[^cite_note-41] and three Visigoth necropolises near Casa de Campo, Tetuán and Vicálvaro.[^cite_note-42]&#x20;

### Middle Ages

The first historical document about the existence of an established settlement in Madrid dates from the Muslim age. In the second half of the 9th century,[^cite_note-43] [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_C%C3%B3rdoba) Emir [Muhammad I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_I_of_C%C3%B3rdoba) built a fortress on a headland near the river [Manzanares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manzanares_\(river\))[^cite_note-44] as one of the many fortresses he ordered built on the border between [Al-Andalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus) and the kingdoms of [León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Le%C3%B3n) and [Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Castile), with the objective of protecting [Toledo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toledo,_Spain) from Christian attacks from the North and as a starting point for Muslim offensives. After the disintegration of the [Caliphate of Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate_of_C%C3%B3rdoba) in the early 11th century, Madrid was integrated in the [Taifa of Toledo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taifa_of_Toledo).[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46]&#x20;

In the context of the wider campaign for the conquest of the taifa of Toledo initiated in 1079, Madrid was seized in 1083 by [Alfonso VI of León and Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_VI_of_Le%C3%B3n_and_Castile), who sought to use the town as an offensive outpost against the city of Toledo,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBahamonde_MagroOtero_Carvajal19899-47] in turn conquered in 1085. Following the conquest, Christians occupied the centre of the city, while Muslims and Jews were displaced to the suburbs. Madrid, located near [Alcalá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcal%C3%A1_de_Henares) (under Muslim control until 1118), remained a borderland for a while, suffering a number of *[razzias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Razzia_\(military\))* during the [Almoravid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almoravid) period, and its walls were destroyed in 1110.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBahamonde_MagroOtero_Carvajal19899-47]&#x20;

Madrid was confirmed as *villa de [realengo](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Realengo_\(legal_status\)\&action=edit\&redlink=1)* (linked to the Crown) in 1123, during the reign of [Alfonso VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_VII_of_Le%C3%B3n_and_Castile).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECestero_ManceraMolina_MartosParedes_García201518-48] The 1123 Charter of Otorgamiento established the first explicit limits between Madrid and Segovia, namely the Puerto de El Berrueco and the Puerto de Lozoya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECerrillo_Torquemada2009245-49] Beginning in 1188, Madrid had the right to be a city with representation in the courts of Castile.[^cite_note-50] In 1202, [Alfonso VIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_VIII_of_Castile) gave Madrid its first charter to regulate the municipal council,[^cite_note-51] which was expanded in 1222 by [Ferdinand III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_III_of_Castile). The government system of the town was changed to a *[regimiento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regimiento)* of 12 *[regidores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regidor)* by [Alfonso XI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_XI_of_Castile) in January 1346.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESuárez_Fernández2001137-52]&#x20;

Starting in the mid-13th century and up to the late 14th century, the *concejo* of Madrid vied for the control of the Real de Manzanares territory against the *concejo* of [Segovia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Segovia), a powerful town north of the [Sierra de Guadarrama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierra_de_Guadarrama) mountain range, characterised by its repopulating prowess and its animal husbandry-based economy, in contrast to the agricultural and less repopulated town of Madrid.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBahamonde_MagroOtero_Carvajal198911–12-53] After the decline of [Sepúlveda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sep%C3%BAlveda,_Segovia), another *concejo* north of the mountain range, Segovia became a major actor south of the Guadarrama mountains, expanding across the [Lozoya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lozoya_\(river\)) and [Manzanares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manzanares_River) rivers to the north of Madrid and along the [Guadarrama river](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadarrama_\(river\)) course to its west.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBahamonde_MagroOtero_Carvajal198911–12-53]&#x20;

In 1309, the Courts of Castile convened at Madrid for the first time under [Ferdinand IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_IV_of_Castile), and later in 1329, 1339, 1391, 1393, 1419 and twice in 1435.&#x20;

### Early Modern Era

During the [revolt of the Comuneros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolt_of_the_Comuneros), led by [Juan López de Padilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_L%C3%B3pez_de_Padilla), Madrid joined the revolt against [Charles, Holy Roman Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor). After defeat at the [Battle of Villalar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Villalar), Madrid was besieged and occupied by the imperial troops. The city was however granted the titles of *Coronada* (Crowned) and *Imperial*.&#x20;



A view of Madrid from the west, facing the Puerta de la Vega. Drawing by [Anton van den Wyngaerde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anton_van_den_Wyngaerde), 1562.



*Baths in the Manzanares in the place of Molino Quemado* (detail), by [Félix Castello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9lix_Castello) (c. 1634–1637)

The number of urban inhabitants grew from 4,060 in 1530 to 37,500 in 1594. The poor population of the court was composed of ex-soldiers, foreigners, and rogues, dissatisfied with the lack of food and high prices. In June 1561 [Phillip II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain) set his court in Madrid, installing it in the old [*alcázar*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Alcazar_of_Madrid).[^cite_note-54] Thanks to this, Madrid became the political centre of the monarchy, being the capital of Spain except for a short period between 1601 and 1606, in which the Court was relocated to [Valladolid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valladolid), and the Madrid population temporarily plummeted. Being the capital was decisive for the evolution of the city and influenced its fate. During the rest of the reign of Philip II, the population boomed, going up from about 18,000 in 1561 to 80,000 in 1598.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParsons200313-55]&#x20;



The [Calle de Alcalá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_de_Alcal%C3%A1) in 1750 by [Antonio Joli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Joli)

In the early 17th century, although Madrid recovered from the loss of its capital status, with the return of diplomats, lords and affluent people, as well as an entourage of noted writers and artists together with them, extreme poverty remained rampant.[^cite_note-56] The century also was a time of heyday for theatre, represented in the so-called [*corrales de comedias*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corral_de_comedias).[^cite_note-57]&#x20;

Madrid changed hands several times during the [War of the Spanish Succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Spanish_Succession): from the Bourbon control it passed to the allied "Austracist" army with Portuguese and English presence that [entered the city in late June 1706](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=First_entry_of_Archduke_Charles\&action=edit\&redlink=1),[^cite_note-58] only to be retaken by the Bourbon army on 4 August 1706.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartínez_Pereira2008180-59] The Habsburg army led by the [Archduke Charles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_VI,_Holy_Roman_Emperor) [entered the city for a second time](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Second_entry_of_Archduke_Charles_in_Madrid\&action=edit\&redlink=1) in September 1710,[^cite_note-60] leaving the city less than three months after. [Philip V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_V_of_Spain) entered the capital on 3 December 1710.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuirós_Rosado2016308-61]&#x20;

Seeking to take advantage of Madrid's location at the geographic centre of Spain, the 18th century saw a sustained effort to create a radial system of communications and transports for the country through public investments.[^cite_note-62]&#x20;

Philip V built the Royal Palace, the [Royal Tapestry Factory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Tapestry_Factory) and the main Royal Academies.[^cite_note-63] The reign of [Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_III_of_Spain), who came to be known as "the best mayor of Madrid", saw an effort to turn the city into a true capital, with the construction of sewers, street lighting, cemeteries outside the city and a number of monuments and cultural institutions. The reforms enacted by his Sicilian minister were however opposed in 1766 by the populace in the so-called [Esquilache Riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esquilache_Riots), a revolt demanding to repeal a clothing decree banning the use of traditional hats and [long cloaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaniard_cloak) aiming to curb crime in the city.[^cite_note-64]&#x20;



*[The Second of May 1808](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Second_of_May_1808)* by [Francisco de Goya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_de_Goya)

Main article: [Dos de Mayo Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dos_de_Mayo_Uprising)

In the context of the [Peninsular War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_War), the situation in French-occupied Madrid after March 1808 was becoming more and more tense. On 2 May, a crowd began to gather near the [Royal Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Palace_of_Madrid) protesting against the French attempt to evict the remaining members of the Bourbon royal family to [Bayonne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayonne), prompting an uprising against the French Imperial troops that lasted hours and spread throughout the city, including a famous last stand at the Monteleón barracks. Subsequent repression was brutal, with many insurgent Spaniards being [summarily executed](1227309.md).[^cite_note-65] The uprising led to a declaration of war calling all the Spaniards to fight against the French invaders.&#x20;

### Capital of the Liberal State



An 1861 map of the *Ensanche de Madrid*

Madrid was invaded on 24 May 1823 by a French army—the so-called [Hundred Thousand Sons of Saint Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hundred_Thousand_Sons_of_Saint_Louis)—called to intervene to restore the [absolutism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy) of Ferdinand that the latter had been deprived from during the [1820–1823 *trienio liberal*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trienio_liberal).[^cite_note-66] Unlike other European capitals, during the first half of the 19th century the only noticeable bourgeois elements in Madrid (that experienced a delay in its industrial development up to that point) were merchants.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarcía_Ruiz2011192-67] The University of Alcalá de Henares was relocated to Madrid in 1836, becoming the [Central University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complutense_University_of_Madrid).[^cite_note-68]&#x20;

Madrid's economy modernised during the second half of the 19th century, consolidating its status as a service and financial centre. New industries were mostly focused in book publishing, construction and low-tech sectors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarcía_Ruiz2011193-69] The introduction of [railway transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway_transport) greatly helped Madrid's economic prowess, and led to changes in consumption patterns, such as the substitution of salted fish for fresh fish from the Spanish coasts, and further strengthening Madrid's role as a [logistics node](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logistics) in Spain's distribution network.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarcía_Ruiz2011194-70] Electric lighting in the streets was introduced in the 1890s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarcía_Ruiz2011194-70]&#x20;

During the first third of the 20th century the population nearly doubled, reaching more than 850,000 inhabitants. New suburbs such as Las Ventas, [Tetuán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetu%C3%A1n,_Madrid) and El Carmen became the homes of the influx of workers, while [Ensanche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ensanche) became a middle-class neighbourhood of Madrid.[^cite_note-71]&#x20;

### Second Republic and Civil War



Children taking refuge during the Francoist bombings (1936–1937) over Madrid during the [Spanish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War)

The [Spanish Constitution of 1931](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Constitution_of_1931) was the first to legislate the location of the country's capital, setting it explicitly in Madrid. During the 1930s, Madrid enjoyed "great vitality"; it was demographically young, becoming urbanised and the centre of new political movements.[^cite_note-72] During this time, major construction projects were undertaken, including the northern extension of the [Paseo de la Castellana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paseo_de_la_Castellana), one of Madrid's major thoroughfares.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMonteroCervera_Gil200916-73] The tertiary sector, including banking, insurance and telephone services, grew greatly.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMonteroCervera_Gil200920-74] Illiteracy rates were down to below 20%, and the city's cultural life grew notably during the so-called *Silver Age* of Spanish Culture; the sales of newspapers also increased.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMonteroCervera_Gil200921-75]&#x20;

The proclamation of the Republic created a severe housing shortage. Slums and squalor grew due to high population growth and the influx of the poor to the city. Construction of affordable housing failed to keep pace and increased political instability discouraged economic investment in housing in the years immediately prior to the Civil War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMonteroCervera_Gil200917;_18-76] Anti-clericalism and Catholicism lived side by side in Madrid; the [burning of convents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burning_of_convents_in_Spain_\(1931\)) initiated after riots in the city in May 1931 worsened the political environment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMonteroCervera_Gil200925;_26-77] However, the [1934 insurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_1934) largely failed in the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMonteroCervera_Gil200926-78]&#x20;

Madrid was one of the most heavily affected cities in the [Spanish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War) (1936–1939). It was a stronghold of the [Republican faction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Spanish_Republic) from July 1936 and became an international symbol of [anti-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-fascism) struggle during the conflict.[^cite_note-79] The city suffered aerial bombing, and in November 1936, its western suburbs were the scene of an all-out battle.[^cite_note-History_of_Madrid_2-80] The city fell to the Francoists in March 1939.&#x20;

See also: [Siege of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Madrid) and [Republican repression in Madrid (1936–1939)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_repression_in_Madrid_\(1936%E2%80%931939\))

### Francoist dictatorship



The [calle de Bravo Murillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_de_Bravo_Murillo) in 1978

A staple of post-war Madrid (*Madrid de la posguerra*) was the widespread use of [ration coupons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationing).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarcía_BallesterosRevilla_González200616-81] Meat and fish consumption was scarce, resulting in high mortality due to malnutrition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarcía_BallesterosRevilla_González200617–18-82] Due to Madrid's history as a left-wing stronghold, the right-wing victors considered moving the capital elsewhere (most notably to [Seville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seville)), but such plans were never implemented. The [Franco regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco_regime) instead emphasised the city's history as the capital of formerly imperial Spain.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFaraldoRodríguez-López2019107–124-83]&#x20;

The intense demographic growth experienced by Madrid via mass immigration from the rural areas of Spain led to the construction of abundant housing in the peripheral areas of Madrid to absorb the new population, initially comprising substandard housing. This increased wealth polarisation in Madrid,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELópez_Simón2018175-84] with as many as 50,000 [shacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shack) scattered around Madrid by 1956.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELópez_Simón2018175;_178-85] A transitional planning intended to temporarily replace the shanty towns were the *poblados de absorción*, introduced since the mid-1950s in locations such as [Canillas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canillas_\(Madrid\)), [San Fermín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Ferm%C3%ADn_\(Madrid\)), Caño Roto, [Villaverde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villaverde_\(Madrid\)), [Pan Bendito](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Pan_Bendito\&action=edit\&redlink=1), [Zofío](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Zof%C3%ADo) and [Fuencarral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuencarral), aiming to work as a sort of "high-end" shacks (with the destinataries participating in the construction of their own housing) but under the aegis of a wider coordinated urban planning.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELópez_Simón2018179–180-86]&#x20;

Madrid grew through the annexation of neighbouring municipalities, achieving the present extent of 607 km<sup>2</sup> (234.36 sq mi). The south of Madrid became heavily industrialised, and there was significant [immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_migration) from rural areas of Spain. Madrid's newly built north-western districts became the home of a newly enriched middle class that appeared as result of the [1960s Spanish economic boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_miracle), while the south-eastern periphery became a large working-class area, which formed the base for active cultural and political movements.[^cite_note-History_of_Madrid_2-80]&#x20;

### Recent history

After the fall of the Francoist regime, the new [1978 constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Spain) confirmed Madrid as the capital of Spain. The [1979 municipal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Madrid_City_Council_election) brought Madrid's first democratically elected mayor since the Second Republic to power.&#x20;

Madrid was the scene of some of the most important events of the time, such as the mass demonstrations of support for democracy after the failed coup, [23-F](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/23-F), on 23 February 1981. The first democratic mayors belonged to the centre-left [PSOE](272596.md) ([Enrique Tierno Galván](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Tierno_Galv%C3%A1n), [Juan Barranco Gallardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Barranco_Gallardo)). Since the late 1970s and through the 1980s Madrid became the centre of the cultural movement known as *[la Movida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movida_madrile%C3%B1a)*. Conversely, just like in the rest of the country, a [heroin crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opioid_epidemic) took a toll in the poor neighbourhoods of Madrid in the 1980s.[^cite_note-87]&#x20;



[2011 Anti-austerity protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-austerity_movement_in_Spain) in the [Puerta del Sol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_del_Sol)

Benefiting from increasing prosperity in the 1980s and 1990s, the capital city of Spain consolidated its position as an important economic, cultural, industrial, educational, and technological centre on the European continent.[^cite_note-History_of_Madrid_2-80] During the mandate as Mayor of [José María Álvarez del Manzano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mar%C3%ADa_%C3%81lvarez_del_Manzano) construction of traffic tunnels below the city proliferated.[^cite_note-88] The following administrations, also conservative, led by [Alberto Ruiz-Gallardón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Ruiz-Gallard%C3%B3n) and [Ana Botella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ana_Botella) launched three unsuccessful bids for the [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics), [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Olympics) and [2020 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-89] In 2005, Madrid was the leading European destination for migrants from [developing countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developing_countries), as well as the largest employer of non-European workforce in Spain.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMasterson-Algar201630-90]&#x20;

In the early years of the 21st century, Madrid experienced the increase of [income inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_inequality) and [socio-economic segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_segregation).[^cite_note-91] Madrid was a centre of the [anti-austerity protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-austerity_movement_in_Spain) that erupted in Spain in 2011.[^cite_note-92] As consequence of the spillover of the [2008 financial and mortgage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis), Madrid has been affected by the increasing number of second-hand homes held by banks and [house evictions](538394.md).[^cite_note-93] The mandate of left-wing Mayor [Manuela Carmena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuela_Carmena) (2015–2019) delivered the renaturalization of the course of the [Manzanares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manzanares_River) across the city.&#x20;

Since the late 2010s, the challenges the city faces include the increasingly unaffordable rental prices (often in parallel with the [gentrification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentrification) and the spike of tourist apartments in the city centre) and the profusion of [betting shops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betting_shop) in working-class areas, leading to an "epidemic" of gambling among young people.[^cite_note-94][^cite_note-elpais-95]&#x20;

## Geography



Madrid as seen by the [Sentinel-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentinel-2) satellite, July 2022

Madrid lies in the centre of the Iberian peninsula on the southern [Meseta Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meseta_Central), 60 kilometres south of the [Guadarrama mountain range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadarrama_mountain_range) and straddling the [Jarama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarama) and [Manzanares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manzanares_\(river\)) river sub-drainage basins, in the wider [Tagus River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagus_River) catchment area. With an average altitude of 650 metres (2,130 ft), Madrid is the second-highest capital of Europe, after [Andorra la Vella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andorra_la_Vella).[^cite_note-96] The difference in altitude within the city proper ranges from the 700 m (2,297 ft) around [Plaza de Castilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Castilla) in the north of city to the 570 m (1,870 ft) around *La China* wastewater treatment plant on the Manzanares' riverbanks, near the latter's confluence with the Fuente Castellana [thalweg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thalweg) in the south of the city.[^cite_note-97]&#x20;

The [Monte de El Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monte_de_El_Pardo), a protected forested area covering over a quarter of the municipality, reaches its top altitude (843 m (2,766 ft)) on its perimeter, in the slopes surrounding [El Pardo reservoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=El_Pardo_reservoir\&action=edit\&redlink=1) located at the north-western end of the municipality, in the [Fuencarral-El Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuencarral-El_Pardo) district.[^cite_note-98]&#x20;

The oldest urban core is located on the hills next to the left bank of the Manzanares River.[^cite_note-plangen-99] Madrid grew to the east, reaching the [Fuente Castellana Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Fuente_Castellana_Creek\&action=edit\&redlink=1), now the [Paseo de la Castellana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paseo_de_la_Castellana), and further east reaching the [Abroñigal Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abro%C3%B1igal_Creek\&action=edit\&redlink=1), now the [M-30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autopista_de_Circunvalaci%C3%B3n_M-30).[^cite_note-plangen-99] Madrid also grew through the annexation of neighbouring urban settlements,[^cite_note-plangen-99] including those to the South West on the right bank of the Manzanares.&#x20;

### Parks and forests

Main article: [List of Madrid parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Madrid_parks)



Main parks in the municipality



[Retiro Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retiro_Park)



The [Manzanares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manzanares_\(river\)) flowing through the [Monte de El Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monte_de_El_Pardo)

Madrid has the second highest number of aligned trees in the world, with 248,000 units, only exceeded by Tokyo. Madrid's citizens have access to a green area within a 15-minute walk. Since 1997, green areas have increased by 16%. At present, 8.2% of Madrid's grounds are green areas, meaning that there are 16 square metres (172 sq ft) of green area per inhabitant, far exceeding the ten-square-metre (110 sq ft) per inhabitant recommended by the World Health Organization.&#x20;

A great bulk of the most important parks in Madrid are related to areas originally belonging to the royal assets (including El Pardo, Soto de Viñuelas, Casa de Campo, El Buen Retiro, la Florida and the [Príncipe Pío hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pr%C3%ADncipe_P%C3%ADo_\(hill\)), and the Queen's Casino).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaliana_Martín199446,_50-100] The other main source for the "green" areas are the *[bienes de propios](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Bienes_de_propios\&action=edit\&redlink=1)* owned by the municipality (including the Dehesa de la Villa, the Dehesa de Arganzuela or Viveros).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaliana_Martín199450-101]&#x20;

[El Retiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buen_Retiro_Park) is the most visited location of the city.[^cite_note-102] Having an area bigger than 1.4 square kilometres (350 acres; 140 hectares; 0.54 sq mi), it is the largest park within the [Almendra Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almendra_Central), the inner part of the city enclosed by the M-30. Created during the reign of [Philip IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_IV_of_Spain) (17th century), it was handed over to the municipality in 1868, after the Glorious Revolution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaliana_Martín199448-103][^cite_note-104] It lies next to the [Royal Botanical Garden of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Jard%C3%ADn_Bot%C3%A1nico_de_Madrid).&#x20;

Located northwest of the city centre, the [Parque del Oeste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_del_Oeste) ("Park of the West") comprises part of the area of the former royal possession of the "Real Florida", and it features a slope as the height decreases down to the Manzanares.[^cite_note-105] Its southern extension includes the [Temple of Debod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Debod), a transported ancient Egyptian temple.[^cite_note-106]&#x20;

Other urban parks are the [Parque de El Capricho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_de_El_Capricho), the [Parque Juan Carlos I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Juan_Carlos_I) (both in northeast Madrid), [Madrid Río](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_R%C3%ADo), the [Enrique Tierno Galván Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Enrique_Tierno_Galv%C3%A1n_Park\&action=edit\&redlink=1), the [San Isidro Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=San_Isidro_Park\&action=edit\&redlink=1) as well as gardens such as the [Campo del Moro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_del_Moro) (opened to the public in 1978)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaliana_Martín199450-101] and the [Sabatini Gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabatini_Gardens) (opened to the public in 1931)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaliana_Martín199450-101] adjacent to the Royal Palace.&#x20;

Further west, across the Manzanares, lies the [Casa de Campo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_de_Campo), a large forested area with more than 1,700 hectares (17 km<sup>2</sup>; 4,200 acres) where the [Madrid Zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoo_Aquarium_de_Madrid), and the [Parque de Atracciones de Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_de_Atracciones_de_Madrid) amusement park are located. It was ceded to the municipality following the proclamation of the Second Spanish Republic in 1931.[^cite_note-107]&#x20;

The [Monte de El Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monte_de_El_Pardo) is the largest forested area in the municipality. A [holm oak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quercus_rotundifolia) forest covering a surface over 16,000 hectares, it is considered the best preserved [mediterranean forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_forest) in the Community of Madrid and one of the best preserved in Europe.[^cite_note-foresta-108] Already mentioned in the [Alfonso XI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_XI_of_Castile)'s *[Libro de la montería](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Libro_de_la_monter%C3%ADa\&action=edit\&redlink=1)* from the mid-14th century, its condition as hunting location linked to the Spanish monarchy help to preserve the environmental value.[^cite_note-foresta-108] During the reign of Ferdinand VII the regime of hunting prohibition for the Monte de El Pardo became one of full property and the expropriation of all possessions within its bounds was enforced, with dire consequences for the madrilenians at the time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaliana_Martín199446-109] It is designated as [Special Protection Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Protection_Area) for bird-life and it is also part of the Regional Park of the High Basin of the Manzanares.&#x20;

Other large forested areas include the [Soto de Viñuelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soto_de_Vi%C3%B1uelas), the [Dehesa de Valdelatas](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dehesa_de_Valdelatas\&action=edit\&redlink=1) and the [Dehesa de la Villa](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dehesa_de_la_Villa\&action=edit\&redlink=1). As of 2015, the most recent big park in the municipality is the Valdebebas Park. Covering a total area of 4.7 km<sup>2</sup> (1.8 sq mi), it is sub-divided in a 3.4-square-kilometre (1.3 sq mi) forest park (the [Parque forestal de Valdebebas-Felipe VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Parque_forestal_de_Valdebebas-Felipe_VI\&action=edit\&redlink=1)), a 0.8-square-kilometre (0.31 sq mi) periurban park as well as municipal garden centres and compost plants.[^cite_note-110]&#x20;

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Madrid)



Madrid during a [rainstorm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainstorm) in spring



The [Royal Palace of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Palace_of_Madrid) under snow, during the Filomena snow storm

Madrid has a [cold semi-arid climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_semi-arid_climate) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) *BSk*), transitioning to a [Mediterranean climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_climate) (Csa) in the western half.[^cite_note-111] Madrid's climate has continental influences.&#x20;

Winters are cool due to its altitude, which is approximately 667 m (2,188 ft) [above sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Above_mean_sea_level) and distance from the moderating effect of the sea. While mostly sunny, rain, sporadic snowfalls and frequent frosts can occur between December and February with cooler temperatures particularly during the night and mornings as cold winds blow into Madrid from surrounding mountains. Summers are hot and sunny. In the warmest month, July, average daytime temperatures range from 32 to 34 °C (90 to 93 °F) depending on location, with maxima commonly climbing over 35 °C (95 °F) and occasionally up to 40 °C during the frequent heat waves. Due to Madrid's altitude and dry climate, humidity is low. [Diurnal ranges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diurnal_temperature_variation) are often significant, particularly on sunny winter days when the temperature rises in the afternoon before rapidly plummeting after nightfall. Madrid is among the sunniest capital cities in Europe.&#x20;

The highest recorded temperature in central Madrid's Retiro Park was on 14 August 2021, with 40.7 °C (105.3 °F). The lowest recorded temperature was on 16 January 1945 with −10.1 °C (13.8 °F) in Madrid.[^cite_note-112] At the airport, on the eastern side of Madrid, the highest recorded temperature was on 14 August 2021, at 42.7 °C (108.9 °F). The lowest recorded temperature was on 16 January 1945 at −15.2 °C (4.6 °F).[^cite_note-113] [From 7 to 9 January 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storm_Filomena), Madrid received the most snow in its recorded history since 1904; Spain's meteorological agency [AEMET](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AEMET) reported between 50 and 60 cm (20 and 24 in) of accumulated snow in its [weather stations](206018.md) within the city.[^cite_note-Pereda-114]&#x20;

[Precipitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precipitation_\(meteorology\)) is typically concentrated in the autumn, winter, and spring. It is particularly sparse during the summer, taking the form of about two showers and/or [thunderstorms](70807.md) during the season. Madrid is the [European capital](9239.md) with the least amount of annual precipitation.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116]&#x20;

At the metropolitan scale, Madrid features both substantial daytime urban cool island and nighttime [urban heat island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_heat_island) effects during the summer season in relation to its surroundings, which feature thinly vegetated dry land.[^cite_note-117]&#x20;

### Water supply



Viaje de Amaniel

In the 17th century, the *viajes de agua* (a kind of water channel or *[qanat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qanat)*) were used to provide water to the city. Some of the most important ones were the [Viaje de Amaniel](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Viaje_de_Amaniel\&action=edit\&redlink=1) (1610–1621, sponsored by the Crown), the [Viaje de Fuente Castellana](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Viaje_de_Fuente_Castellana\&action=edit\&redlink=1) (1613–1620) and [Abroñigal Alto](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abro%C3%B1igal_Alto\&action=edit\&redlink=1)/[Abroñigal Bajo](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abro%C3%B1igal_Bajo\&action=edit\&redlink=1) (1617–1630), sponsored by the City Council. They were the main infrastructure for the supply of water until the arrival of the [Canal de Isabel II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_de_Isabel_II) in the mid-19th century.[^cite_note-124]&#x20;

Madrid derives almost 73.5 percent of its [water supply](684928.md) from dams and reservoirs built on the [Lozoya River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lozoya_\(river\)), such as the [El Atazar Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Atazar_Dam).[^cite_note-125] This water supply is managed by the Canal de Isabel II, a public entity created in 1851. It is responsible for the supply, depurating waste water and the conservation of all the natural water resources of the Madrid region.&#x20;

As of 2025, the population is 3,477,497, of which 46.9% are males, and 53.1% are females. Minors make up 14.6% of the population, and seniors make up 20.2%.[^cite_note-population-2]&#x20;

The population of Madrid has overall increased since the city became the capital of Spain in the mid-16th century, and has stabilised at around 3 million since the 1970s.[^cite_note-historicalpopulation-126]&#x20;

From 1970 until the mid-1990s, the population dropped. This phenomenon, which also affected other European cities, was caused in part by the growth of satellite suburbs at the expense of the downtown region within the city proper. This also occurred during a period of slowed growth in the European economy.&#x20;

The [demographic boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_growth) accelerated in the late-1990s and early first decade of the 21st century due to [immigration](4599312.md) in parallel with a surge in Spanish [economic growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_growth).&#x20;

The wider Madrid region is the EU region with the highest average life expectancy at birth. The average life expectancy was 82.2 years for males and 87.8 for females in 2016.[^cite_note-127]&#x20;

According to [Eurostat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostat), the "metropolitan region" of Madrid has a population of slightly more than 6.271 million people[^cite_note-Eurostat-128] covering an area of 4,609.7 km<sup>2</sup> (1,780 sq mi). It is the largest in Spain and the [second largest in the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_in_Europe_by_population).[^cite_note-demographia-3][^cite_note-citypopulation.de-14][^cite_note-un.org-15]&#x20;

### Immigration

As the capital city of Spain, the city has attracted many immigrants from around the world, with most of the immigrants coming from [Latin American](18524.md) countries. As of 2025, immigrants make up 29.2% of the population – the majority of them originating from [the Americas](29833.md) (around 21.2% of the total population), with a lesser fraction of the population from other European, Asian and African countries. The 5 largest foreign countries of birth are [Venezuela](32374.md), [Colombia](5222.md), [Peru](170691.md), [Ecuador](9334.md), and the [Dominican Republic](8060.md).[^cite_note-population-2] As of 2025, the fastest-growing groups of immigrants are Colombians, [Mexicans](3966054.md), [Gambians](65656.md) and Venezuelans, all having increased by over 65% since 2021.[^cite_note-population-2]&#x20;

As for foreign citizens, as of 2025, they make up 18.8% of the population. The 5 largest foreign countries of citizenship are [Venezuela](32374.md), [Colombia](5222.md), [Peru](170691.md), [China](5405.md), and [Italy](14532.md).[^cite_note-population-2]&#x20;

### Religion

Most people in Madrid are [Roman Catholic Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church). It is the seat of the [Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Madrid). In a 2011 survey conducted by InfoCatólica, 63.3% of Madrid residents of all ages identified themselves as [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic).[^cite_note-cbn.com-129]&#x20;

According to a 2019 [Centro de Investigaciones Sociológicas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_de_Investigaciones_Sociol%C3%B3gicas) (CIS) survey with a sample size of 469 respondents, 20.7% of respondents in Madrid identify themselves as practising Catholics, 45.8% as non-practising Catholics, 3.8% as believers of another religion, 11.1% as [agnostics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnosticism), 3.6% as indifferent towards religion, and 12.8% as [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism). The remaining 2.1% did not state their religious beliefs.[^cite_note-130]&#x20;

## Government

Main article: [City Council of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Council_of_Madrid)

See also: [List of mayors of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mayors_of_Madrid)

### Local government and administration

Main article: [City Council of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Council_of_Madrid)



The façade of the city hall



A plenary session of the city council

The City Council (*Ayuntamiento de Madrid*) is the body responsible for the government and administration of the municipality. It is formed by the Plenary (*Pleno*), the Mayor (*alcalde*) and the Government Board (*Junta de Gobierno de la Ciudad de Madrid*).&#x20;

The Plenary of the Ayuntamiento is the body of [political representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representation_\(politics\)) of the citizens in the [municipal government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government). Its 57 members are elected for a 4-year mandate. Some of its attributions are: fiscal matters, the election and deposition of the mayor, the approval and modification of decrees and regulations, the approval of budgets, the agreements related to the limits and alteration of the municipal term, the services management, the participation in supramunicipal organisations, etc.[^cite_note-131]&#x20;

The mayor, the supreme representative of the city, presides over the [*Ayuntamiento*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayuntamiento_\(Spain\)). He is charged with giving impetus to the municipal policies, managing the action of the rest of bodies and directing the executive municipal administration.[^cite_note-132] He is responsible to the *Pleno*. He is also entitled to preside over the meetings of the *Pleno*, although this responsibility can be delegated to another municipal councillor. [José Luis Martínez-Almeida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Luis_Mart%C3%ADnez-Almeida), a member of the [People's Party](404727.md), has served as mayor since 2019.&#x20;

The Government Board consists of the mayor, deputy mayors and a number of delegates assuming the portfolios for the different government areas. All those positions are held by municipal councillors.[^cite_note-133]&#x20;

Since 2007, the [Cybele Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cybele_Palace) (or Palace of Communications) serves as [City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Hall).&#x20;

### Capital of Spain



[Moncloa Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moncloa_Palace), seat of the [President of the Government of Spain](203556.md)

Madrid is the capital of Spain. The [King of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Spain), the country's head of state, has his official residence in the [Zarzuela Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Zarzuela). As the seat of the [Government of Spain](1306113.md), Madrid houses the official residence of the [President of the Government](203556.md) (Prime Minister) and regular meeting place of the [Council of Ministers](1287120.md), the [Moncloa Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Moncloa), and the headquarters of the ministerial departments.&#x20;

Both the residences of the head of state and government are located in the northwest of Madrid. The seats of the Lower and Upper Chambers of the Spanish Parliament, the Cortes Generales (respectively, the [Palacio de las Cortes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_de_las_Cortes) and the [Palacio del Senado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_del_Senado)), are in Madrid.&#x20;

### Regional capital

Madrid is the capital of the Community of Madrid. The region has its own legislature and enjoys a wide range of competencies in areas such as social spending, healthcare, and education. The seat of the regional parliament, the [Assembly of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembly_of_Madrid), is located at the district of [Puente de Vallecas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_de_Vallecas). The [presidency of the regional government](608649.md) is headquartered at the [Royal House of the Post Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_House_of_the_Post_Office) at the very centre of the city, the [Puerta del Sol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_del_Sol).&#x20;

### Law enforcement



Municipal police agents from the 2018 promotion

The [Madrid Municipal Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polic%C3%ADa_Municipal_de_Madrid) (*Policía Municipal de Madrid*) is the local law enforcement body, dependent on the *Ayuntamiento*. In 2018, it had a workforce of 6,190 civil servants.[^cite_note-134]&#x20;

The headquarters of both the [Directorate-General of the Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directorate-General_of_the_Police) and the [Directorate-General of the Civil Guard](62445177.md) are located in Madrid. The headquarters of the Higher Office of Police of Madrid (*Jefatura Superior de Policía de Madrid*), the peripheral branch of the [National Police Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuerpo_Nacional_de_Polic%C3%ADa) with jurisdiction over the region also lies in Madrid.&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Madrid)



The [Madrid Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsa_de_Madrid)

After it became the capital of Spain in the 16th century, Madrid was more a centre of [consumption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumption_\(economics\)) than of production or trade. Economic activity was largely devoted to supplying the city's own rapidly growing population, including the royal household and national government, and to such trades as [banking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank) and [publishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publishing).&#x20;

A large [industrial sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_sector) did not develop until the 20th century, but thereafter industry greatly expanded and diversified, making Madrid the second industrial city in Spain. However, the economy of the city is now becoming more and more dominated by the [service sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector_of_the_economy). A major European financial centre, its stock market is the third largest stock market in Europe featuring both the [IBEX 35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IBEX_35) index and the attached [Latibex](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Latibex\&action=edit\&redlink=1) stock market (with the second most important index for [Latin American](18524.md) companies).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoreno-Fernández202045-22]&#x20;

Madrid is the 5th most important leading Centre of Commerce in Europe (after London, Paris, Frankfurt and Amsterdam) and ranks 11th in the world.[^cite_note-mastercard.com-136] It is the leading Spanish-speaking city in terms of webpage creation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoreno-Fernández202045-22]&#x20;

### Economic history

As the capital city of the [Spanish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire) from 1561, Madrid's population grew rapidly. Administration, banking, and small-scale manufacturing centred on the royal court were among the main activities, but the city was more a locus of consumption than production or trade, geographically isolated as it was before the coming of the railways.&#x20;

The [Bank of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Spain) is one of the oldest European central banks. Originally named as the Bank of San Carlos as it was founded in 1782, it was later renamed to Bank of San Fernando in 1829 and ultimately became the Bank of Spain in 1856.[^cite_note-137] [Its headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Spain_Building) are located at the [calle de Alcalá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_de_Alcal%C3%A1). The [Madrid Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsa_de_Madrid) was inaugurated on 20 October 1831.[^cite_note-138] Its benchmark stock market index is the [IBEX 35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IBEX_35).&#x20;

Industry started to develop on a large scale only in the 20th century,[^cite_note-Historia-139] but then grew rapidly, especially during the "[Spanish miracle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_miracle)" period around the 1960s. The economy of the city was then centred on manufacturing industries such as those related to [motor vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motor_vehicle), aircraft, chemicals, electronic devices, pharmaceuticals, [processed food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_processing), printed materials, and leather goods.[^cite_note-Overview:_Economy-140] Since the restoration of democracy in the late 1970s, the city has continued to expand. Its economy is now among the most dynamic and diverse in the [European Union](9317.md).[^cite_note-141]&#x20;

### Present-day economy



[Telefónica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telef%C3%B3nica) headquarters

Madrid concentrates activities directly connected with power (central and regional government, headquarters of Spanish companies, regional HQ of [multinationals](214491.md), [financial institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finance)) and with knowledge and technological innovation (research centres and universities). It is one of Europe's largest financial centres, and the largest in Spain.[^cite_note-Estructura-142] The city has 17 universities and over 30 research centres.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 52 </sup> It is the second metropolis in the EU by population, and the third by gross internal product.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 69 </sup> Leading employers include [Telefónica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telef%C3%B3nica), [Iberia](98613.md), [Prosegur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosegur), [BBVA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBVA), Urbaser, [Dragados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragados), and [FCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fomento_de_Construcciones_y_Contratas).[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 569 </sup>&#x20;

The [Community of Madrid](69802.md), the region comprising the city and the rest of municipalities of the province, had a [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP) of [€](1797747.md)220B in 2017, equating to a GDP per capita of €33,800.[^cite_note-143] In 2011 the city itself had a GDP per capita 74% above the national average and 70% above that of the 27 [European Union](9317.md) member states, although 11% behind the average of the top 10 cities of the EU.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 237–239 </sup> Although housing just over 50% of the [region](69802.md)'s population, the city generates 65.9% of its GDP.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 51 </sup> Following the [recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008%E2%80%9314_Spanish_financial_crisis) commencing 2007/8, recovery was under way by 2014, with forecast growth rates for the city of 1.4% in 2014, 2.7% in 2015 and 2.8% in 2016.[^cite_note-Barometro-144]<sup>: 10 </sup>&#x20;

The economy of Madrid has become based increasingly on the [service sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector_of_the_economy). In 2011 services accounted for 85.9% of value added, while [industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_sector) contributed 7.9% and [construction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Construction) 6.1%.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 51 </sup> Nevertheless, Madrid continues to hold the position of Spain's second industrial centre after Barcelona, specialising particularly in high-technology production. Following the recession, services and industry were forecast to return to growth in 2014, and construction in 2015.[^cite_note-Barometro-144]<sup>: 32 </sup>&#x20;

#### Standard of living

Mean household income and spending are 12% above the Spanish average.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 537, 553 </sup> The proportion classified as "at risk of poverty" in 2010 was 15.6%, up from 13.0% in 2006 but less than the average for Spain of 21.8%. The proportion classified as affluent was 43.3%, much higher than Spain overall (28.6%).[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 540–3 </sup>&#x20;

Consumption by Madrid residents has been affected by job losses and by [austerity](684037.md) measures, including a rise in sales tax from 8% to 21% in 2012.[^cite_note-145]&#x20;

Although residential property prices have fallen by 39% since 2007, the average price of dwelling space was €2,375.6 per sq. m. in early 2014,[^cite_note-Barometro-144]<sup>: 70 </sup> and is shown as second only to London in a list of 22 European cities.[^cite_note-146]&#x20;

#### Employment

Participation in the labour force was 1,638,200 in 2011, or 79.0%. The employed workforce comprised 49% women in 2011 (Spain, 45%).[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 98 </sup> 41% of economically active people are university graduates, against 24% for Spain as a whole.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 103 </sup>&#x20;

In 2011, the unemployment rate was 15.8%, remaining lower than in Spain as a whole. Among those aged 16–24, the unemployment rate was 39.6%.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 97, 100 </sup> Unemployment reached a peak of 19.1% in 2013,[^cite_note-Barometro-144]<sup>: 17 </sup> but with the start of an economic recovery in 2014, employment started to increase.[^cite_note-147] Employment continues to shift further towards the service sector, with 86% of all jobs in this sector by 2011, against 74% in all of Spain.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 117 </sup> In the second quarter of 2018 the unemployment rate was 10.06%.[^cite_note-148]&#x20;



Headquarters of the [Bank of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Spain)

#### Services



[Mercamadrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercamadrid) facilities in South-Eastern Madrid

The share of services in the city's economy is 86%. Services for business, transport & communications, property, and finance together account for 52% of the total value added.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 51 </sup> The types of services that are now expanding are mainly those that facilitate movement of capital, information, goods and persons, and "advanced business services" such as [research and development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_development) (R\&D), [information technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_technology), and technical [accountancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accountancy).[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 242–3 </sup>&#x20;

Madrid and the wider region's authorities have put a notable effort in the development of [logistics infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logistics_center). Within the city proper, some of the standout centres include [Mercamadrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercamadrid), the [Madrid-Abroñigal](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Madrid-Abro%C3%B1igal_Station\&action=edit\&redlink=1) logistics centre, the Villaverde's Logistics Centre and the Vicálvaro's Logistics Centre to name a few.[^cite_note-149]&#x20;

[Banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank) based in Madrid carry out 72% of the banking activity in Spain.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 474 </sup> The Spanish [central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_bank), [Bank of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Spain), has existed in Madrid since 1782. Stocks & shares, [bond](60737.md) markets, [insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurance), and [pension funds](43615.md) are other important forms of financial institution in the city.&#x20;

Madrid is an important centre for [trade fairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_fair), many of them coordinated by [IFEMA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IFEMA), the Trade Fair Institution of Madrid.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 351–2 </sup> The [public sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_sector) employs 18.1% of all employees.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 630 </sup> Madrid attracts about 8M [tourists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism) annually from other parts of Spain and from all over the world, exceeding even [Barcelona](4443.md).[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 81 </sup>[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 362, 374 </sup>[^cite_note-Barometro-144]<sup>: 44 </sup> Spending by tourists in Madrid was estimated (2011) at €9,546.5M, or 7.7% of the city's GDP.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 375 </sup>&#x20;

The construction of [transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Madrid) infrastructure has been vital to maintain the economic position of Madrid. Travel to work and other local journeys use a high-capacity metropolitan road network and a well-used [public transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transport) system.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 62–4 </sup> In terms of longer-distance transport, Madrid is the central node of the system of *[autovías](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autov%C3%ADa)* and of the [high-speed rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail) network ([AVE](523382.md)), which has brought major cities such as Seville and Barcelona within 2.5 hours travel time.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 72–75 </sup> Also important to the city's economy is [Madrid-Barajas Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid-Barajas_Airport), the fourth largest airport in Europe.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 76–78 </sup> Madrid's central location makes it a major [logistical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logistics) base.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 79–80 </sup>&#x20;

#### Industry



[PSA Peugeot Citroën](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groupe_PSA) plant in [Villaverde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villaverde_\(Madrid\)) district

As an industrial centre Madrid retains its advantages in infrastructure, as a transport hub, and as the location of headquarters of many companies. Industries based on advanced technology are acquiring much more importance here than in the rest of Spain.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 271 </sup> Industry contributed 7.5% to Madrid's value-added in 2010.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 265 </sup> However, industry has slowly declined within the city boundaries as more industry has moved outward to the periphery. Industrial Gross Value Added grew by 4.3% in the period 2003–2005, but decreased by 10% during 2008–2010.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 271, 274 </sup> The leading industries were: paper, printing & publishing, 28.8%; energy & mining, 19.7%; vehicles & transport equipment, 12.9%; electrical and electronic, 10.3%; foodstuffs, 9.6%; clothing, footwear & textiles, 8.3%; chemical, 7.9%; industrial machinery, 7.3%.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 266 </sup>&#x20;

The [PSA Peugeot Citroën](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groupe_PSA) plant is located in [Villaverde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villaverde_\(Madrid\)) district.&#x20;

#### Construction



Building works of [Caleido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caleido) in August 2018

The construction sector, contributing 6.5% to the city's economy in 2010,[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 265 </sup> was a growing sector before the recession, aided by a large transport and infrastructure program. More recently the construction sector has fallen away and earned 8% less in 2009 than it had been in 2000.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 242–3 </sup> The decrease was particularly marked in the residential sector, where prices dropped by 25%–27% from 2007 to 2012/13[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 202, 212 </sup> and the number of sales fell by 57%.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 216 </sup>&#x20;

#### Tourism



Fitur fair in Ifema

Madrid is the seat of the [World Tourism Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Tourism_Organization) (UNWTO) and the [International Tourism Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=International_Tourism_Fair\&action=edit\&redlink=1) (FITUR).&#x20;

In 2018, the city received 10.21 million tourists (53.3% of them international tourists).[^cite_note-MADdestino-150]<sup>p. 9</sup> The biggest share of international tourists come from the United States, followed by Italy, France, United Kingdom and Germany.[^cite_note-MADdestino-150]<sup>p. 10</sup> As of 2018, the city has 793 hotels, 85,418 hotel places and 43,816 hotel rooms.[^cite_note-MADdestino-150]<sup>p. 18</sup> It also had, as of 2018, an estimated 20,217 tourist apartments.[^cite_note-MADdestino-150]<sup>p. 20</sup>&#x20;

The most visited museum was the *[Museo Nacional Centro de Arte Reina Sofía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Nacional_Centro_de_Arte_Reina_Sof%C3%ADa)*, with 3.8 million visitors in the sum of its three seats in 2018. Conversely, the [Prado Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prado_Museum) had 2.8 million visitors and the [Thyssen-Bornemisza Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thyssen-Bornemisza_Museum) 906,815 visitors.[^cite_note-MADdestino-150]<sup>p. 32</sup>&#x20;

By the late 2010s, the gentrification and the spike of tourist apartments in the city centre led to an increase in rental prices, pushing residents out of the city centre.[^cite_note-elpais-95] Most of the tourist apartments in Madrid (50–54%) are located in the Centro District.[^cite_note-eldiario-151] In the [Sol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sol_\(Madrid\)) neighbourhood (part of the latter district), 3 out of 10 homes are dedicated to tourist apartments,[^cite_note-eldiario-151] and 2 out of 10 are listed in [AirBnB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AirBnB).[^cite_note-elpais-95] In April 2019 the plenary of the *ayuntamiento* passed a plan intending to regulate this practice, seeking to greatly limit the number of tourist apartments. The normative would enforce a requirement for independent access to those apartments on and off the street.[^cite_note-152] However, after the change of government in June 2019, the new municipal administration planned to revert the regulation.[^cite_note-153]&#x20;

#### International rankings

A recent study placed Madrid 7th among 36 cities as an attractive base for business.[^cite_note-154] It was placed third in terms of availability of office space, and fifth for ease of access to markets, availability of qualified staff, mobility within the city, and quality of life. Its less favourable characteristics were seen as pollution, languages spoken, and political environment. Another ranking of European cities placed Madrid 5th among 25 cities (behind [Berlin](3354.md), London, Paris and [Frankfurt](10992.md)), being rated favourably on economic factors and the labour market as well as transport and communication.[^cite_note-155]&#x20;

### Media and entertainment

The Madrid metropolitan area is an important film and television production hub, whose content is distributed throughout the [Spanish-speaking world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish-speaking_world) and abroad. It is often seen as the entry point into the European media market for [Latin American](18524.md) media companies, and likewise the entry point into the Latin American markets for European companies.[^cite_note-156] It is also the headquarters of media groups such as [Radiotelevisión Española](1014212.md) (RTVE), [Atresmedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atresmedia), [Mediaset España](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediaset_Espa%C3%B1a), and [Movistar+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movistar%2B), which produce numerous films, television shows and series which are distributed globally on various platforms.[^cite_note-157] Since 2018, it is also home to [Netflix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netflix)'s Madrid Production Hub, [Mediapro Studio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediapro), and numerous others such as [Viacom International Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viacom_International_Media_Networks).[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] As of 2019, the film and television industry employs 19,000 people locally (44% of people in Spain working in this industry).[^cite_note-162]&#x20;



[TVE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Televisi%C3%B3n_Espa%C3%B1ola)'s central news services are located at the foot of [Torrespaña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torrespa%C3%B1a).

The [Torrespaña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torrespa%C3%B1a) [broadcasting tower](2044496.md), located in Madrid's [Salamanca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salamanca_\(Madrid\)) district, is the central and main transmission node of the terrestrial broadcasting network in Spain. RTVE, the state-owned radio and television [public broadcaster](178808.md) is [headquartered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prado_del_Rey_\(studios\)) in [Pozuelo de Alarcón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pozuelo_de_Alarc%C3%B3n) along with all its channels and web services ([La 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_1_\(Spanish_TV_channel\)), [La 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_2_\(Spanish_TV_channel\)), [Clan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clan_\(TV_channel\)), [Teledeporte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teledeporte), [24 Horas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/24_Horas_\(Spanish_TV_channel\)), [TVE Internacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TVE_Internacional), [Radio Nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Nacional_\(Spanish_radio_station\)), [Radio Exterior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Exterior), and [Radio Clásica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Cl%C3%A1sica)). Atresmedia group ([Antena 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antena_3_\(Spain\)), [La Sexta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Sexta), [Onda Cero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onda_Cero)) is headquartered in [San Sebastián de los Reyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Sebasti%C3%A1n_de_los_Reyes). [Mediaset España](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediaset_Espa%C3%B1a) ([Telecinco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecinco), [Cuatro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuatro_\(TV_channel\))) maintains its headquarters in Madrid's [Fuencarral-El Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuencarral-El_Pardo) district. Together with RTVE, Atresmedia and Mediaset account for nearly the 80% of share of [generalist television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generalist_channel).[^cite_note-enguix-163] The Spanish media conglomerate [PRISA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PRISA) ([Cadena SER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadena_SER), [Los 40 Principales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_40_Principales), [M80 Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M80_Radio), [Cadena Dial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadena_Dial)) is headquartered in [Gran Vía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gran_V%C3%ADa,_Madrid) street in central Madrid.&#x20;

Besides hosting the main television and radio producers and broadcasters, the metropolitan area hosts most of the major written mass media in Spain,[^cite_note-enguix-163] including *[ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_\(newspaper\))*, *[El País](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Pa%C3%ADs)*, *[El Mundo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Mundo_\(Spain\))*, *[La Razón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Raz%C3%B3n_\(Madrid\))*, *[Marca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marca_\(newspaper\))*, *[¡Hola!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C2%A1Hola!)*, *[Diario AS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diario_AS)*, *[El Confidencial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Confidencial)* and *[Cinco Días](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinco_D%C3%ADas)*. The Spanish international news agency [EFE](387067.md) maintains its headquarters in Madrid since its inception in 1939. The second news agency of Spain is the privately owned [Europa Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europa_Press_\(news_agency\)), founded and headquartered in Madrid since 1953.&#x20;

## Culture

### Architecture

Main article: [Architecture of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Madrid)

| icon | This section **needs [more citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability)**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Madrid) by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Referencing_for_beginners). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *(January 2015)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Little medieval architecture is preserved in Madrid, mostly in the [Almendra Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almendra_Central), including the [San Nicolás](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_San_Nicol%C3%A1s_\(Madrid\)) and [San Pedro el Viejo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Pedro_el_Real,_Madrid) church towers, the church of [San Jerónimo el Real](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Jer%C3%B3nimo_el_Real), and the [Bishop's Chapel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapel_of_Obispo_de_Madrid). Nor has Madrid retained much Renaissance architecture, other than the [Bridge of Segovia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridge_of_Segovia_\(Madrid\)) and the [Convent of Las Descalzas Reales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convent_of_Las_Descalzas_Reales).&#x20;



The [Plaza Mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_Mayor,_Madrid), built in the 16th century

Philip II moved his court to Madrid in 1561 and transformed the town into a capital city. During the Early Habsburg period, the import of European influences took place, underpinned by the monicker of *Austrian style*. The Austrian style features Austrian, Italian, Dutch and Spanish influences, reflecting on the international preeminence of the Habsburgs.[^cite_note-164] During the second half of the 16th century, the use of pointy slate [spires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spire) in order to top structures such as church towers was imported to Spain from Central Europe.[^cite_note-165] Slate spires and roofs consequently became a staple of the Madrilenian architecture at the time.[^cite_note-lib-166]&#x20;

Stand out architecture in the city dating back to the early 17th century includes several buildings and structures, most of them attributed to [Juan Gómez de Mora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_G%C3%B3mez_de_Mora) such as the [Palace of the Duke of Uceda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_de_los_Concejos) (1610), the [Monastery of La Encarnación](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Monastery_of_La_Encarnaci%C3%B3n) (1611–1616); the [Plaza Mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_Mayor_de_Madrid) (1617–1619) or the *Cárcel de Corte* (1629–1641), known as the [Santa Cruz Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz_Palace,_Madrid).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlasco_Esquivias2016280-167] The 1600s saw the construction of the former City Hall, the [Casa de la Villa](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Casa_de_la_Villa\&action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEscobar200760–63-168]&#x20;

The [Imperial College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colegio_Imperial_de_Madrid) church model dome was imitated in all of Spain. [Pedro de Ribera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_de_Ribera) introduced [Churrigueresque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Churrigueresque) architecture to Madrid. The [Cuartel del Conde-Duque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuartel_del_Conde-Duque), the [church of Montserrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Montserrat_Church,_Madrid), and the [Bridge of Toledo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridge_of_Toledo_\(Madrid\)) are among the best examples.&#x20;



The [Royal Palace of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Palace_of_Madrid), built in the 18th century

The reign of [the Bourbons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourbon_Spain) during the eighteenth century marked a new era in Madrid. [Philip V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_V_of_Spain) tried to complete King Philip II's vision of urbanisation of Madrid. Philip V built a palace in line with French taste, and buildings such as [St. Michael's Basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Michael%27s_Basilica_\(Madrid\)) and the [Church of Santa Bárbara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Santa_Barbara,_Madrid). King [Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_III_of_Spain) beautified the city and endeavoured to convert Madrid into one of the great European capitals.
