He pushed forward the construction of the Prado Museum (originally intended as a Natural Science Museum), the [Puerta de Alcalá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_de_Alcal%C3%A1), the [Royal Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Observatory_of_Madrid), the [Basilica of San Francisco el Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_San_Francisco_el_Grande), the Casa de Correos in [Puerta del Sol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerta_del_Sol), the [Real Casa de la Aduana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Casa_de_la_Aduana), and the General Hospital, which now houses the Reina Sofia Museum and Royal Conservatory of Music. The [Paseo del Prado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paseo_del_Prado), surrounded by gardens and decorated with neoclassical statues, is an example of urban planning. The [Duke of Berwick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_of_Berwick) ordered the construction of the [Liria Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liria_Palace).&#x20;

In the early 19th century, the [Peninsular War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_War), the [loss of viceroyalties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_American_wars_of_independence) in the Americas, and continuing coups limited the city's architectural development. The [Royal Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Real), the [National Library of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblioteca_Nacional_de_Espa%C3%B1a), the Palace of the Senate, and the [Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Deputies_\(Spain\)#Congress_of_Deputies_building) were built in this era. The [Segovia Viaduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Segovia_Viaduct) linked the Royal Alcázar to the southern part of town.&#x20;



The [Círculo de Bellas Artes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%ADrculo_de_Bellas_Artes)

A list of key figures of madrilenian architecture during the 19th and 20th centuries includes authors such as [Narciso Pascual y Colomer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narciso_Pascual_Colomer), [Francisco Jareño y Alarcón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Jare%C3%B1o_y_Alarc%C3%B3n), [Francisco de Cubas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_de_Cubas), [Juan Bautista Lázaro de Diego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Bautista_L%C3%A1zaro_de_Diego), [Ricardo Velázquez Bosco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Vel%C3%A1zquez_Bosco), [Antonio Palacios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Palacios), [Secundino Zuazo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secundino_Zuazo), [Luis Gutiérrez Soto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Guti%C3%A9rrez_Soto), [Luis Moya Blanco](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Luis_Moya_Blanco\&action=edit\&redlink=1) and [Alejandro de la Sota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_de_la_Sota_\(architect\)).[^cite_note-169]&#x20;

From the mid-19th century until the Civil War, Madrid modernised and built new neighbourhoods and monuments. The expansion of Madrid developed under the [Plan Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ensanche), resulting in the neighbourhoods of [Salamanca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salamanca_\(Madrid\)), [Argüelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moncloa-Aravaca), and [Chamberí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber%C3%AD). [Arturo Soria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arturo_Soria) conceived the [linear city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linear_city_\(Soria_design\)) and built the first few kilometres of the road that bears his name, which embodies the idea. The [Gran Vía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gran_V%C3%ADa_\(Madrid\)) was built using different styles that evolved over time: French style, eclectic, art deco, and expressionist.&#x20;

[Art Nouveau in Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau_in_Madrid), known as *Modernismo* was developed at the turn of the century, in concert with its appearance elsewhere in Europe, including [Barcelona](4443.md) and [Valencia](63861.md). [Antonio Palacios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Palacios) built a series of buildings inspired by the [Viennese Secession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viennese_Secession), such as the [Palace of Communication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Communication), the [Círculo de Bellas Artes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%ADrculo_de_Bellas_Artes), and the [Río de La Plata Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%ADo_de_La_Plata_Bank) (now [Instituto Cervantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Cervantes)). Other notable buildings include the [Bank of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Spain), the neo-Gothic [Almudena Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almudena_Cathedral), [Atocha Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atocha_Station), and the Catalan art-nouveau [Palace of Longoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Longoria). [Las Ventas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Ventas) Bullring was built, and the [Market of San Miguel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_of_San_Miguel).&#x20;



The [Edificio España](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edificio_Espa%C3%B1a)

Following the Francoist takeover that ensued the end of Spanish Civil war, architecture experienced an involution, discarding rationalism and, eclecticism notwithstanding, going back to an overall rather "outmoded" architectural language, with the purpose of turning Madrid into a capital worthy of the "Immortal Spain".[^cite_note-torrus-170] Iconic examples of this period include the [Ministry of the Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Headquarters_of_the_Air_and_Space_Force) (a case of [herrerian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herrerian) revival) and the [Edificio España](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edificio_Espa%C3%B1a), presented as the tallest building in Europe when it was inaugurated in 1953.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-torrus-170] Many of these buildings distinctly combine the use of brick and stone in the façades.[^cite_note-torrus-170] The [Casa Sindical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_Sindical) marked a breaking point as it was the first to reassume rationalism, although that relinking to modernity was undertaken through the imitation of the Italian Fascist architecture.[^cite_note-torrus-170]&#x20;

In the late 20th century, with the advent of Spanish economic development, skyscrapers, such as [Torre Picasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_Picasso), Torres Blancas and Torre BBVA, and the [Gate of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gate_of_Europe) were built. In the 2000s, the four tallest skyscrapers in Spain were built and together form the [Cuatro Torres Business Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuatro_Torres_Business_Area).[^cite_note-172] Terminal 4 at [Madrid-Barajas Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid-Barajas_Airport) was inaugurated in 2006 and won several architectural awards. Terminal 4 is one of the world's largest terminal areas[^cite_note-173] and features glass panes and domes in the roof, which allow natural light to pass through.&#x20;

### Museums and cultural centres

See also: [List of museums in Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_museums_in_Madrid)



*[Las Meninas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Meninas)*, by [Diego Velázquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez), 1656, [Prado Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prado_Museum)

Madrid is considered one of the top European destinations concerning art museums. Best known is the [Golden Triangle of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Triangle_of_Art), located along the [Paseo del Prado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paseo_del_Prado) and comprising three major museums: the [Prado Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_del_Prado), the [Reina Sofía Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Nacional_Centro_de_Arte_Reina_Sof%C3%ADa), and the [Thyssen-Bornemisza Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thyssen-Bornemisza_Museum).&#x20;

The Prado Museum (*Museo del Prado*) is a museum and art gallery that features one of the world's finest collections of European art, from the 12th century to the early 19th century, based on the former [Spanish Royal Collection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Royal_Collection). It has the best collection of artworks by [Goya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Goya), [Velázquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez), [El Greco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Greco), [Rubens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubens), [Titian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titian), [Hieronymus Bosch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hieronymus_Bosch), [José de Ribera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_Ribera), and [Patinir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patinir), as well as works by [Rogier van der Weyden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogier_van_der_Weyden), [Raphael Sanzio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raphael_Sanzio), [Tintoretto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tintoretto), [Veronese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paolo_Veronese), [Caravaggio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caravaggio), [Van Dyck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Van_Dyck), [Albrecht Dürer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albrecht_D%C3%BCrer), [Claude Lorrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Lorrain), [Murillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bartolom%C3%A9_Esteban_Murillo), and [Zurbarán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurbar%C3%A1n), among others. Some of the standout works exhibited at the museum include *[Las Meninas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Meninas)*, *[La maja vestida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_maja_vestida)*, *[La maja desnuda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_maja_desnuda)*, *[The Garden of Earthly Delights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Garden_of_Earthly_Delights)*, *[The Immaculate Conception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Immaculate_Conception_\(Tiepolo\))* and *[The Judgement of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Judgement_of_Paris_\(Rubens\))*.&#x20;

The Thyssen-Bornemisza Museum (*Museo Thyssen-Bornemisza*) is an art museum that fills the historical gaps in its counterparts' collections: in the Prado's case, this includes Italian primitives and works from the [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_art), [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_School_\(painting\)), and German schools, while in the case of the Reina Sofía, the Thyssen-Bornemisza collection, once the second largest private collection in the world after the British [Royal Collection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Collection),[^cite_note-nyt-obit-174] includes [Impressionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impressionist), [Expressionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expressionist), and European and American paintings from the second half of the 20th century, with over 1,600 paintings.[^cite_note-175]&#x20;



[Queen Sofía National Museum Art Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Nacional_Centro_de_Arte_Reina_Sof%C3%ADa)

The Reina Sofía National Art Museum (*Museo Nacional Centro de Arte Reina Sofía*; MNCARS) is Madrid's national museum of [20th-century art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th-century_art) and houses [Pablo Picasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Picasso)'s 1937 anti-war masterpiece, *[Guernica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guernica_\(painting\))*. Other highlights of the museum, which is mainly dedicated to Spanish art, include excellent collections of Spain's greatest 20th-century masters including [Salvador Dalí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Dal%C3%AD), [Joan Miró](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_Mir%C3%B3), Picasso, [Juan Gris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Gris), and [Julio González](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julio_Gonz%C3%A1lez_\(sculptor\)). The Reina Sofía also hosts a free-access art library.[^cite_note-176]&#x20;



A cloister in the [National Archaeological Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Archaeological_Museum_\(Madrid\)) (MAN) showcasing prehistoric items from the Iberian Peninsula, including the [Mausoleum of Pozo Moro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mausoleum_of_Pozo_Moro)

The [National Archaeological Museum of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Archaeological_Museum_\(Madrid\)) (*Museo Arqueológico Nacional*) shows archaeological finds from [Prehistory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory) to the 19th century, including Roman mosaics, Greek ceramics, Islamic art and Romanesque art, especially from the Iberian Peninsula, distributed over three floors. An iconic item in the museum is the [Lady of Elche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lady_of_Elche), an Iberian bust from the 4th century BC. Other major pieces include the [Lady of Baza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lady_of_Baza), the [Lady of Cerro de los Santos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lady_of_Cerro_de_los_Santos), the [Lady of Ibiza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lady_of_Ibiza), the [Bicha of Balazote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicha_of_Balazote), the [Treasure of Guarrazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treasure_of_Guarrazar), the [Pyxis of Zamora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyxis_of_Zamora), the [Mausoleum of Pozo Moro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mausoleum_of_Pozo_Moro) and a [napier's bones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napier%27s_bones). In addition, the museum has a reproduction of the polychromatic paintings in the [Altamira Cave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altamira_Cave).&#x20;

The [Royal Academy of Fine Arts of San Fernando](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Academia_de_Bellas_Artes_de_San_Fernando) (*Real Academia de Bellas Artes de San Fernando*) houses a fine art collection of paintings ranging from the 15th to 20th centuries. The academy is the headquarters of the Madrid Academy of Art.[^cite_note-179]&#x20;

[CaixaForum Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CaixaForum_Madrid) is a post-modern art gallery in the centre of Madrid, next to the Prado Museum.[^cite_note-180]&#x20;

The [Royal Palace of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Palace_of_Madrid), a massive building characterised by its luxurious rooms, houses rich collections of armours and weapons, as well as the most comprehensive collection of [Stradivarius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Stradivari) in the world.[^cite_note-181] The [Museo de las Colecciones Reales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_de_las_Colecciones_Reales) is a future museum intended to host the most outstanding pieces of the Royal Collections part of the [Patrimonio Nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrimonio_Nacional). Located next to the Royal Palace and the Almudena, Patrimonio Nacional has tentatively scheduled its opening for 2021.[^cite_note-182]&#x20;



The [Museo de América](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_de_Am%C3%A9rica)

The [Museum of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_the_Americas_\(Madrid\)) (*Museo de América*) is a national museum that holds artistic, archaeological, and ethnographic collections from the Americas, ranging from the [Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleolithic) period to the present day.[^cite_note-museodeamerica1-183]&#x20;

Other notable museums include the [National Museum of Natural Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Nacional_de_Ciencias_Naturales) (the Spain's national museum of [natural history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_history)),[^cite_note-autogenerated2-184] the [Naval Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Naval_de_Madrid),[^cite_note-185] the [Convent of Las Descalzas Reales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convent_of_Las_Descalzas_Reales) (with many works of Renaissance and Baroque art, and [Brussels tapestries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_tapestry) inspired by paintings of Rubens),[^cite_note-patrimonionacional1-186] the [Museum of Lázaro Galdiano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_L%C3%A1zaro_Galdiano) (housing a collection specialising in decorative arts, featuring a collection of weapons that features the sword of Pope [Innocent VIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innocent_VIII)),[^cite_note-autogenerated3-187] and the [National Museum of Decorative Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Nacional_de_Artes_Decorativas).[^cite_note-188]&#x20;

Institutions include the [National Museum of Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Romanticism_\(Madrid\)) (focused on 19th century Romanticism),[^cite_note-189] the [Museum Cerralbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_Cerralbo),[^cite_note-190] the [National Museum of Anthropology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Nacional_de_Antropolog%C3%ADa_\(Madrid\)) (featuring as highlight a [Guanche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanches) mummy from [Tenerife](269223.md)),[^cite_note-191] the [Sorolla Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorolla_Museum) (focused in the namesake Valencian Impressionist painter,[^cite_note-museosorolla1-192] also including sculptures by [Auguste Rodin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_Rodin), part of Sorolla's personal effects),[^cite_note-museosorolla2-193] or the [History Museum of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_de_Historia_de_Madrid) (housing pieces related to the local history of Madrid), the [Wax Museum of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wax_Museum_of_Madrid), and the [Railway Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway_Museum_\(Madrid\)) (located in the building that was once the Delicias Station).&#x20;

Major cultural centres in the city include the [Fine Arts Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%ADrculo_de_Bellas_Artes) (one of Madrid's oldest arts centres and one of the most important private cultural centres in Europe, hosting exhibitions, shows, film screenings, conferences and workshops), the [Conde Duque cultural centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuartel_del_Conde-Duque) or the [Matadero Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matadero_Madrid), a cultural complex (formerly an abattoir) located by the river Manzanares. The Matadero, created in 2006 with the aim of "promoting research, production, learning, and diffusion of creative works and contemporary thought in all their manifestations", is considered the third most valued cultural institution in Madrid among art professionals.[^cite_note-194]&#x20;

### Language

The usual language in Madrid is [Peninsular Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_Spanish). It is in the transition between northern and southern dialects. Typical features are:&#x20;

- *[Yeísmo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ye%C3%ADsmo)*, *calló* and *cayó* sound alike among all social classes.

  [^cite_note-Zamora-195]

  &#x20;According to [Alonso Zamora Vicente](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alonso_Zamora_Vicente\&action=edit\&redlink=1), *yeísmo* has extended from Madrid across Spain.

  [^cite_note-Zamora-195]

- Aspiration of [coda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syllabic_coda) /s/.

  [^cite_note-Quilis-196]

- Frequent elision of final /d/ ([\[maˈðɾi\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Spanish)) and devoicing /θ/ ([\[maˈðɾiθ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Spanish) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/d/d0/ES-pe\_-\_Madrid.ogg/ES-pe\_-\_Madrid.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/d/d0/ES-pe_-_Madrid.ogg/ES-pe_-_Madrid.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>) coexist with the standard preservation ([\[maˈðɾið\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Spanish) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/7/75/Pronunciation_of_Madrid_in_Spanish.ogg/Pronunciation_of_Madrid_in_Spanish.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/7/75/Pronunciation_of_Madrid_in_Spanish.ogg/Pronunciation_of_Madrid_in_Spanish.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>) realised with varying degrees of relaxation.

  [^cite_note-Molina_Martos_2016-9]

- *[Leísmo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le%C3%ADsmo)*, *[laísmo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La%C3%ADsmo)* and *[loísmo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lo%C3%ADsmo)*. According to [Samuel Gili Gaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Samuel_Gili_Gaya\&action=edit\&redlink=1), in Madrid speech, pronoun *le* is specialised in the masculine and pronoun *la* in the feminine, for direct and indirect objects.

  [^cite_note-Gili-197]

The arrival to Madrid of a substantial number of immigrants from [Latin America](18524.md) (such as [Ecuadorians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecuadorians)) has induced processes of dialectal convergence and divergence in the city.[^cite_note-198]&#x20;

In the 1970s and 1980s, Madrid youth created their own slang, [Cheli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheli).[^cite_note-Buzek-199]&#x20;

### Literature

Madrid has been one of the great centres of [Spanish literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_literature). Some of the most distinguished writers of the [Spanish Golden Century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Golden_Age) were born in Madrid, including [Lope de Vega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lope_de_Vega) (author of *[Fuenteovejuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuenteovejuna)* and *[The Dog in the Manger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Dog_in_the_Manger_\(play\))*), who reformed the Spanish theatre, a project continued by [Calderon de la Barca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calderon_de_la_Barca) (author of *[Life is a Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_is_a_Dream)*). [Francisco de Quevedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_de_Quevedo), who criticised the Spanish society of his day, and author of *[El Buscón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Busc%C3%B3n)*, and [Tirso de Molina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tirso_de_Molina), who created the character [Don Juan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Juan), were born in Madrid. [Cervantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_de_Cervantes) and [Góngora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_de_G%C3%B3ngora) also lived in the city, although they were not born there. The Madrid homes of Lope de Vega, Quevedo, Gongora, and Cervantes still exist, and they are all in the Barrio de las Letras (Literary Neighbourhood). Other writers born in Madrid in later centuries include [Leandro Fernandez de Moratín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leandro_Fernandez_de_Morat%C3%ADn), [Mariano José de Larra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariano_Jos%C3%A9_de_Larra), [Jose de Echegaray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_de_Echegaray) ([Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md)), [Ramón Gómez de la Serna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram%C3%B3n_G%C3%B3mez_de_la_Serna), [Dámaso Alonso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A1maso_Alonso), [Enrique Jardiel Poncela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Jardiel_Poncela) and [Pedro Salinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Salinas).&#x20;

The "Barrio de las Letras" owes its name to the intense literary activity taking place there during the 16th and 17th centuries. Some of the most prominent writers of the [Spanish Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Golden_Age) lived here, such as [Lope de Vega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lope_de_Vega), [Quevedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_de_Quevedo), and [Góngora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_de_G%C3%B3ngora), and it contained the Cruz and Príncipe Theatres, two of the most important in Spain. At 87 Calle de Atocha, on the northern end of the neighbourhood, was the printing house of [Juan de la Cuesta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_la_Cuesta), where the first edition of [Don Quixote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Quixote) was typeset and printed in 1604. Most of the literary routes are articulated along the Barrio de las Letras, where you can find scenes from novels of the [Siglo de Oro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siglo_de_Oro) and more recent works like "[Bohemian Lights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohemian_Lights)". Although born in [Las Palmas de Gran Canaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Palmas_de_Gran_Canaria), [realist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Realist_literature) writer [Benito Pérez Galdós](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_P%C3%A9rez_Gald%C3%B3s) made Madrid the setting for many of his stories; there is a giidebook to the Madrid of Galdós (*Madrid galdosiano*).[^cite_note-200]&#x20;



Interior of the National Library of Spain

Madrid is home to the [Royal Spanish Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Spanish_Academy), the Royal Academy of the Spanish Language, which governs, with statutory authority, over Spanish,[^cite_note-201] preparing, publishing, and updating authoritative reference works on it. The academy's motto (*lema*, in Spanish) states its purpose: it cleans the language, stabilises it, and gives it brilliance ("Limpia, fija y da resplendor"). Madrid is home to another international cultural institution, the [Instituto Cervantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Cervantes), whose task is the promotion and teaching of the Spanish language as well as the dissemination of the culture of Spain and [Hispanic America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_America). The [National Library of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblioteca_Nacional_de_Espa%C3%B1a) is the largest major public library in Spain. The library's collection has more than 26,000,000 items, including 15,000,000 books and other printed materials, 30,000 manuscripts, 143,000 newspapers and serials, 4,500,000 graphic materials, 510,000 music scores, 500,000 maps, 600,000 sound recording, 90,000 audiovisuals, 90,000 electronic documents, and more than 500,000 microforms.[^cite_note-202]&#x20;

### Cuisine



Three [squid sandwiches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squid_sandwich)



*[Patatas bravas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patatas_bravas)*, a very common bar snack served as *[tapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapas)*

The Madrilenian cuisine has received plenty of influences from other regions of Spain and its own identity actually relies in its ability to assimilate elements from the immigration.[^cite_note-5d-203]&#x20;

The *[cocido madrileño](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocido_madrile%C3%B1o)*, a [chickpea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chickpea)-based [stew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stew), is one of the most emblematic dishes of the Madrilenian cuisine.[^cite_note-204] The *[callos a la madrileña](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Callos_a_la_madrile%C3%B1a\&action=edit\&redlink=1)* is another traditional winter specialty, usually made of cattle [tripes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripe).[^cite_note-Madridiario-2020-205] Other [offal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offal) dishes typical in the city include the *[gallinejas](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Gallinejas\&action=edit\&redlink=1)*[^cite_note-Madridiario-2020-205] or grilled [pig's ear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pig%27s_ear_\(food\)).[^cite_note-206] Fried [squid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squid) has become a culinary specialty in Madrid, often consumed in sandwich as [*bocata de calamares*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squid_sandwich).[^cite_note-Madridiario-2020-205]&#x20;

Other generic dishes commonly accepted as part of the Madrilenian cuisine include the *[potaje](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potaje)*, the *[sopa de ajo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garlic_soup)* (Garlic soup), the [Spanish omelette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_omelette), the *[besugo a la madrileña](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Besugo_a_la_madrile%C3%B1a\&action=edit\&redlink=1)* ([bream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bream)), *[caracoles a la madrileña](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Caracoles_a_la_madrile%C3%B1a\&action=edit\&redlink=1)* ([snails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snail), sp. *[Cornu aspersum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornu_aspersum)*) or the [soldaditos de Pavía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soldaditos_de_Pav%C3%ADa), the *[patatas bravas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patatas_bravas)* (consumed as snack in bars) or the *gallina en [pepitoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Pepitoria\&action=edit\&redlink=1)* (hen or chicken cooked with the yolk of [hard-boiled eggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boiled_egg) and [almonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almond)) to name a few.[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-davalos-208][^cite_note-5d-203]&#x20;

Traditional desserts include *[torrijas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_toast)* (a variant of French toast consumed during Easter)[^cite_note-Madridiario-2020-205][^cite_note-209] and *[bartolillos](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Bartolillos\&action=edit\&redlink=1)*.[^cite_note-davalos-208]&#x20;

See also: [Cuisine of the Community of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuisine_of_the_Community_of_Madrid)

### Nightlife



Nightlife in the Centro District

Madrid is an international hub of highly active, diverse [nightlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nightlife) with [bars](272207.md), dance bars and [nightclubs](19321330.md) staying open past midnight.[^cite_note-210] Madrid is known to have a "vibrant [nightlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nightlife)".[^cite_note-cnn-211] Some of the more popular locations include the surroundings of the [Plaza de Santa Ana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Santa_Ana), [Malasaña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malasa%C3%B1a) and La Latina (particularly near the [Cava Baja](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Calle_de_la_Cava_Baja\&action=edit\&redlink=1)).[^cite_note-cnn-211] It is one of the city's main attractions with tapas bars, cocktail bars, clubs, jazz lounges, live music venues and flamenco theatres. Most nightclubs liven up by 1\:30 a.m. and stay open until at least 6 a.m.[^cite_note-cnn-211]&#x20;

Nightlife flourished in the 1980s while Madrid's mayor [Enrique Tierno Galván](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Tierno_Galv%C3%A1n) ([PSOE](272596.md)) was in office, nurturing the cultural-musical movement known as *[La Movida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Movida_Madrile%C3%B1a)*.[^cite_note-212] Nowadays, the [Malasaña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malasa%C3%B1a) area is known for its [alternative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_music) scene.&#x20;

The area of [Chueca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chueca,_Madrid) has also become a hot spot in the Madrilenian nightlife, especially for the gay population. Chueca is known as gay quarter, comparable to the [Castro District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castro_District,_San_Francisco) in San Francisco.[^cite_note-213]&#x20;

See also: [La Movida Madrileña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Movida_Madrile%C3%B1a)

### Bohemian culture



Nights in [Malasaña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malasa%C3%B1a) are often crowded.

The city has venues for performing alternative art and expressive art. They are mostly located in the centre of the city, including in Ópera, Antón Martín, [Chueca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chueca,_Madrid) and [Malasaña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malasa%C3%B1a). There are also several festivals in Madrid, including the Festival of Alternative Art, and the Festival of the Alternative Scene.[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217]&#x20;

The neighbourhood of [Malasaña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malasa%C3%B1a), as well as Antón Martín and [Lavapiés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavapi%C3%A9s), hosts several bohemian cafés/galleries. These cafés are typified with period or retro furniture or furniture found on the street, a colourful, nontraditional atmosphere inside, and usually art displayed each month by a new artist, often for sale. Cafés include the retro café *Lolina* and bohemian cafés *La Ida*, *La Paca* and *Café de la Luz* in Malasaña, *La Piola* in Huertas and *Café Olmo* and *Aguardiente* in Lavapiés.&#x20;

In the neighbourhood of Lavapiés, there are also "hidden houses", which are illegal bars or abandoned spaces where concerts, poetry readings and[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] the famous Spanish *[botellón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botell%C3%B3n)* (a street party or gathering that is now illegal but rarely stopped).&#x20;

### Classical music and opera



The [Teatro Real](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Real)

The [Auditorio Nacional de Música](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Auditorium_of_Music) [^cite_note-221] is the main venue for classical music concerts in Madrid. It is home to the [Spanish National Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_National_Orchestra), the [Chamartín Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamart%C3%ADn_Symphony_Orchestra)[^cite_note-Orquesta_Sinfónica_Chamartín-Historia_(in_Spanish)-222] and the venue for the symphonic concerts of the [Community of Madrid Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_of_Madrid_Orchestra) and the [Madrid Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Symphony_Orchestra). It is also the principal venue for orchestras on tour playing in Madrid.&#x20;

The [Teatro Real](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Real) is the main opera house in Madrid, located just in front of the [Royal Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Palace_of_Madrid), and its resident orchestra is the [Madrid Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Symphony_Orchestra).[^cite_note-223] The theatre stages around seventeen opera titles (both own productions and co-productions with other major European opera houses) per year, as well as two or three major ballets and several recitals.&#x20;

The [Teatro de la Zarzuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_de_la_Zarzuela) is mainly devoted to [Zarzuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zarzuela) (the Spanish traditional musical theatre genre), as well as [operetta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operetta) and [recitals](236918.md#Recital).[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225] The resident orchestra of the theatre is the [Community of Madrid Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_of_Madrid_Orchestra).&#x20;

The [Teatro Monumental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Monumental) is the concert venue of the [RTVE Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RTVE_Symphony_Orchestra).[^cite_note-226]&#x20;

Other concert venues for classical music are the Fundación Joan March and the [Auditorio 400](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Nacional_Centro_de_Arte_Reina_Sof%C3%ADa), devoted to contemporary music.&#x20;

### Feasts and festivals

#### San Isidro



Festivities of San Isidro Labrador in the *pradera*, 2007

The local feast (*[fiesta patronal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiesta_patronal)*) observed with a day off if falling on a [work day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Work_day) is the [Feast of Saint Isidore the Laborer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feast_of_Saint_Isidore_the_Laborer) (*Fiestas de San Isidro Labrador*), the patron Saint of Madrid, celebrated every 15 May. It is a [public holiday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holiday). According to tradition, [Isidro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Isidore_the_Laborer) was a farmworker and well manufacturer born in Madrid in the late 11th century, who lived a pious life and whose corpse was reportedly found to be [incorrupt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incorruptibility) in 1212. Already very popular among the Madrilenian people, as Madrid became the capital of the Hispanic Monarchy in 1561 the town council pulled efforts to promote his canonisation; the process started in 1562.[^cite_note-227] Isidro was beatified in 1619 and the feast day set on 15 May[^cite_note-228] (he was finally canonised in 1622).[^cite_note-229]&#x20;

On 15 May the Madrilenian people gather around the [Hermitage of San Isidro](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Hermitage_of_San_Isidro\&action=edit\&redlink=1) and the [Prairie of San Isidro](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Park_of_San_Isidro\&action=edit\&redlink=1) (on the right-bank of the [Manzanares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manzanares_River)) often dressed with checkered caps (*[parpusas](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Parpusa\&action=edit\&redlink=1)*) and kerchiefs (*safos*)[^cite_note-230] characteristic of the [chulapos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chulapo) and chulapas, dancing *[chotis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schottische)* and *[pasodobles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasodoble)*, eating *rosquillas* and *barquillos*.[^cite_note-231]&#x20;

#### LGBT pride

Main article: [Madrid Pride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Pride)



High heels race in WorldPride Madrid 2017

The Madrilenian LGBT Pride has become a popular event in the community both in the city and worldwide.[^cite_note-232]&#x20;

Madrid's Pride Parade began in 1977, in the Chueca neighbourhood, which also marked the beginning of the gay, lesbian, transgender, and bisexual rights movement after being repressed for forty years in a dictatorship.[^cite_note-MADO-2017-233] This claiming of LGBT rights has allowed the Pride Parade in Madrid to grow year after year, becoming one of the best in the world. In 2007, this was recognised by the European Pride Organisers Association (EPOA) when Madrid hosted [EuroPride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroPride). It was hailed by the then President of the EPOA as "the best EuroPride in history".[^cite_note-MADO-2016-234]&#x20;

In 2017, Madrid celebrated the 40th anniversary of their first Pride Parade by hosting the WorldPride Madrid 2017. Numerous conferences, seminars and workshops as well as cultural and sports activities took place at the festival, the event being a "kids and family pride" and a source of education. More than one million people attended the pride's central march.[^cite_note-235] The main purpose of the celebration was presenting Madrid and the Spanish society in general as a multicultural, diverse, and tolerant community.[^cite_note-MADO-2017-233] The 2018 [Madrid Pride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Pride) roughly had 1.5 million participants.[^cite_note-MADdestino-150]<sup>p. 34</sup>&#x20;

Since Spain legalised [same-sex marriage](92656.md) in July 2005,[^cite_note-236] Madrid has become one of the largest hot spots for LGBT culture. With about 500 businesses aimed toward the LGBT community, Madrid has become a "Gateway of Diversity".[^cite_note-MADO-2016-234]&#x20;

#### Other



People in costumes during the proclamation (*pregón*) of the 2013 Carnival

Despite often being labelled as "having no tradition" by foreigners,[^cite_note-alpuente-237] the [Carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnival) was popular in Madrid already in the 16th century. However, during the Francoist dictatorship the carnival was under government ban and the feasts suffered a big blow.[^cite_note-alpuente-237][^cite_note-238] It has been slowly recovering since then.&#x20;

Other signalled days include the regional day (2 May) commemorating the [Dos de Mayo Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dos_de_Mayo_Uprising) (a public holiday), the feasts of San Antonio de la Florida (13 June), the feast of the Virgen de la Paloma (circa 15 August) or the day of the co-patron of Madrid, the [Virgin of Almudena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_of_Almudena) (9 November), although the latter's celebrations are rather religious in nature.[^cite_note-239]&#x20;

The most important musical event in the city is the [Mad Cool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mad_Cool) festival; created in 2016, it reached an attendance of 240,000 during the three-day long schedule of the 2018 edition.[^cite_note-MADdestino-150]<sup>p. 33</sup>&#x20;

### Bullfighting



View of Las Ventas bullring in Madrid from [Calle de Alcalá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_de_Alcal%C3%A1)

Madrid hosts the largest *plaza de toros* (bullring) in Spain, [Las Ventas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Ventas), established in 1929. Las Ventas is considered by many to be the world centre of bullfighting and has a [seating capacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seating_capacity) of almost 25,000. Madrid's [bullfighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bullfighting) season begins in March and ends in October. Bullfights are held every day during the festivities of [San Isidro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isidore_the_Laborer) (Madrid's [patron saint](68055.md)) from mid May to early June, and every Sunday, and [public holiday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holiday), the rest of the season. The style of the plaza is [Neo-Mudéjar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Mud%C3%A9jar). Las Ventas also hosts music concerts and other events outside of the bullfighting season. There is great controversy in Madrid with bullfighting.[^cite_note-240]&#x20;

### Sport

Main article: [Sport in Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Madrid)

#### Football

Main article: [Football in Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Madrid)



The [Madrid Derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Derby) at the [Santiago Bernabéu Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_Bernab%C3%A9u_Stadium), January 2015

[Real Madrid](26413.md), founded in 1902, compete in [La Liga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Liga) and play their home games at the [Santiago Bernabéu Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_Bernab%C3%A9u_Stadium). The club is one of the most widely supported teams in the world and their supporters are referred to as *Madridistas* or *Merengues* (Meringues). Real's supporters in Madrid are often believed to be constituted principally of members of the middle classes, however, this claim is in dispute and has not been proved. It has also been suggested that a large proportion of Real Madrid's fans are members of the working class.[^cite_note-241] The club was selected as the [best club of the 20th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA_Club_of_the_Century), being the fifth [most valuable sports club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes_list_of_the_most_valuable_sports_teams) in the world and the [most successful Spanish football club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_football_clubs_in_Spain_by_major_honours_won) with a total of 104 official titles (this includes a record 15 [European Cups](44220.md) and a record 36 La Liga trophies).&#x20;

[Atlético Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atl%C3%A9tico_Madrid), founded in 1903, also compete in La Liga and play their home games at the [Metropolitano Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitano_Stadium). The club is well-supported in the city, having the third national fan base in Spain and their supporters are referred to as *Atléticos* or *Colchoneros* (The Mattressers). Atlético is believed to draw its support mostly from working class citizens.[^cite_note-242] The club is considered an [elite European team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_coefficient#Men's_club_coefficient), having won three [UEFA Europa League](232175.md) titles and reached three [European Cup finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_Cup_and_UEFA_Champions_League_finals). Domestically, Atletico have won eleven league titles and ten [Copa del Reys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copa_del_Rey).&#x20;

[Rayo Vallecano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rayo_Vallecano), founded in 1924, are the third most important football team of the city, based in the [Vallecas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vallecas) neighbourhood. They currently compete in La Liga, having secured promotion in 2021. The club's fans tend to be very left-wing and are known as *[Buccaneers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buccaneer)*.&#x20;

[Getafe CF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Getafe_CF), founded in 1983, also compete in La Liga and play their home games at the [Estadio Coliseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estadio_Coliseum). The club was promoted to La Liga for the first time in 2004, and participated in the top level of Spanish football for twelve years between 2004 and 2016, and again since 2017.&#x20;

[CD Leganés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CD_Legan%C3%A9s), founded in 1928, compete in [Segunda División](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Segunda_Divisi%C3%B3n) and play their home games at the [Estadio Municipal de Butarque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estadio_Municipal_de_Butarque). In the 2015–16 season, for the first time in their history, Leganés earned promotion to La Liga. They remained in the top flight for four seasons, reaching a peak of 13th in 2018–19, before relegation in the last game of the following season, a 2–2 home draw with Real Madrid.&#x20;

Madrid has hosted five European Cup/Champions League finals: four at the Santiago Bernabéu, and the [2019 final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_UEFA_Champions_League_Final) at the Metropolitano. The Bernabéu also hosted the [Euro 1964 Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_European_Nations%27_Cup_Final) (which [Spain](609825.md) won) and the [1982 FIFA World Cup Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_FIFA_World_Cup_Final).&#x20;

#### Basketball



The [2014 FIBA Basketball World Cup Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_FIBA_Basketball_World_Cup_Final) at the [Palacio de Deportes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_de_Deportes_de_la_Comunidad_de_Madrid)

[Real Madrid Baloncesto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Madrid_Baloncesto), founded in 1931, compete in [Liga ACB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liga_ACB) and play their home games at the [Palacio de Deportes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_de_Deportes_de_la_Comunidad_de_Madrid) (WiZink Center). Real Madrid's basketball section, similar to its football team, is the most successful team in Europe, with a record 11 [EuroLeague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroLeague) titles. Domestically, they have clinched a record 36 league titles and a record 28 [Copa del Reys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copa_del_Rey_de_Baloncesto).&#x20;

[Club Baloncesto Estudiantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CB_Estudiantes), founded in 1948, compete in [LEB Oro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liga_Espa%C3%B1ola_de_Baloncesto) and also play their home games at the Palacio de Deportes (WiZink Center). Until 2021, Estudiantes was one of only three teams that have never been relegated from [Spain's top division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_basketball_league_system). Historically, its achievements include three cup titles and four league runners-up placements.&#x20;

Madrid has hosted six [European Cup/EuroLeague finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroLeague_Finals), the last two at the Palacio de Deportes. The city also hosted the final matches for the [1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_FIBA_World_Championship) and [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_FIBA_Basketball_World_Cup) [FIBA World Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_Basketball_World_Cup), and the [EuroBasket 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroBasket_2007) final (all held at the Palacio de Deportes).&#x20;

#### Events



The [2009 Madrid Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Mutua_Madrile%C3%B1a_Madrid_Open_%E2%80%93_Women%27s_Singles) Women's Final at the [Caja Mágica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caja_M%C3%A1gica)

The main annual international event in [cycling](857311.md), the [Vuelta a España](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vuelta_a_Espa%C3%B1a) (La Vuelta), is one of the three worldwide prestigious three-week-long [Grand Tours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Tours), and its final stages takes place in Madrid on the first Sunday of September. In [tennis](29773.md), the city hosts [Madrid Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Open_\(tennis\)), both male and female versions, played on clay court. The event is part of the nine [ATP Masters 1000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ATP_Tour_Masters_1000) and nine [WTA 1000 tournaments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTA_1000_tournaments). It is held during the first week of May in the [Caja Mágica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caja_M%C3%A1gica). Additionally, Madrid hosts the finals of the major tournament for men's national teams, [Davis Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davis_Cup), since [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Davis_Cup).&#x20;

#### Formula One

In January 2024, [Formula One](10854.md) announced that Madrid will host the 2026 [Spanish Grand Prix](526356.md) around the [IFEMA exhibition centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Recinto_Ferial_de_IFEMA\&action=edit\&redlink=1) in [Campo de las Naciones](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Campo_de_las_Naciones\&action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-BBCMadridAnnouncement2-244] It was later confirmed that the [FIA Formula 2 Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIA_Formula_2_Championship) and [FIA Formula 3 Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIA_Formula_3_Championship) would also race that weekend.[^cite_note-245]&#x20;

## Education

Main article: [Education in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Spain)

[Education in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Spain) is free, and compulsory from 6 to 16 years. The [education system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education) is called LOE (*Ley Orgánica de Educación*).[^cite_note-246]&#x20;

### Universities

Madrid is home to many public and [private universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_university). Some of them are among the oldest in the world, and many of them are the most prestigious universities in Spain.&#x20;

The [National Distance Education University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universidad_Nacional_de_Educaci%C3%B3n_a_Distancia) (*Universidad Nacional de Educación a Distancia*; UNED) has as its mission the public service of higher education through the modality of distance education. At more than 205,000 students (2015), UNED has the largest student population in Spain and is one of the largest universities in Europe. Since 1972, UNED has sought to translate into action the principle of equal opportunity in access to higher education through a methodology based on the principles of distance learning and focused on the needs of the student.[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249]&#x20;



The rectorate of the [Complutense University of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complutense_University_of_Madrid)

The [Complutense University of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complutense_University_of_Madrid) (*Universidad Complutense de Madrid*; UCM) is the second largest university in Spain after UNED and one of the oldest universities in the world. It has over 11,000 staff members and a student population of 117,000. Most of the academic staff is Spanish. It is located on two campuses, the main one of [Ciudad Universitaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciudad_Universitaria_de_Madrid) in the [Moncloa-Aravaca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moncloa-Aravaca) district, and the secondary campus of Somosaguas, located outside the city limits in [Pozuelo de Alarcón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pozuelo_de_Alarc%C3%B3n) and founded in 1971.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-Universidad_Complutense-251]&#x20;

The Complutense University of Madrid was founded in [Alcalá de Henares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcal%C3%A1_de_Henares), old Complutum, by Cardinal Cisneros in 1499. Its real origin dates back to 1293, when King [Sancho IV of Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sancho_IV_of_Castile) built the General Schools of Alcalá, which would give rise to Cisnero's Complutense University. Between 1509 and 1510, five schools were already operative: *Artes y Filosofía* (Arts and Philosophy), *Teología* (Theology), *Derecho Canónico* (Canonical Laws), *Letras* (Liberal Arts) and *Medicina* (Medicine). In 1836, during the reign of [Isabel II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabella_II), the university was moved to Madrid, where it took the name of Central University and was located at San Bernardo Street.&#x20;

In 1927, a new [University City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_City_of_Madrid) (Ciudad Universitaria) was planned to be built in the district of Moncloa-Aravaca, in lands handed over by the King [Alfonso XIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_XIII) to this purpose. The [Spanish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War) turned the University City into a war zone, causing the destruction of several schools in the area, as well as the loss of part of its rich scientific, artistic and bibliographic heritage. In 1970 the Government reformed higher education, and the Central University became the Complutense University of Madrid. A new campus at Somosaguas was created to house the new School of Social Sciences. In 1977, the old Alcalá campus was reopened as the independent UAH, [University of Alcalá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alcal%C3%A1). Complutense also serves a population of students who select Madrid for their [study abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Study_abroad) period. Students from the United States, for example, might go to Madrid on a program like API (Academic Programs International) and study at Complutense for an intense immersion in the Spanish language. After studying at the university, students return home with fluency in Spanish and an enhanced understanding of culture and diversity.[^cite_note-Universidad_Complutense_de_Madrid-252]&#x20;



The School of Mines, [Technical University of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technical_University_of_Madrid)

The [Technical University of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technical_University_of_Madrid) (*Universidad Politécnica de Madrid*; UPM), is the top technical university in Spain. It is the result of the merger of different [Technical Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technical_school) of Engineering. It shares the Ciudad Universitaria campus with the UCM, and owns several schools scattered in the city centre and campuses in the [Puente de Vallecas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_de_Vallecas) district and in the neighbouring municipality of [Boadilla del Monte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boadilla_del_Monte).&#x20;

The [Autonomous University of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_University_of_Madrid) (*Universidad Autónoma de Madrid*; UAM) was instituted under the leadership of the physicist, [Nicolás Cabrera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_Cabrera_\(physicist\)). The Autonomous University is widely recognised for its research strengths in [theoretical physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theoretical_physics). Known simply as *La Autónoma* by locals, its main site is the Cantoblanco Campus, located in the north of the municipality, close to its boundaries with the neighbouring municipalities of [Alcobendas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcobendas), [San Sebastián de los Reyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Sebasti%C3%A1n_de_los_Reyes) and [Tres Cantos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tres_Cantos).&#x20;

Located on the main site are the Rectorate building and the Faculties of Science, Philosophy and [Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fine_art), Law, [Economic Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economics) and [Business Studies](39206.md), Psychology, Higher School of [Computing Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_science) and Engineering, and the Faculty of [Teacher Training](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teacher_education) and Education. The UAM is considered the institution to study law in Spain,[^cite_note-Elmundo.es-253] The [Medical School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_school) is outside the main site and beside the Hospital Universitario La Paz.[^cite_note-Universidad_Autónoma-254]&#x20;

The private [Comillas Pontifical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comillas_Pontifical_University) (*Universidad Pontificia Comillas*, UPC) has its rectorate and several faculties in Madrid. The private [Nebrija University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebrija_University) is also based in Madrid. Some of the large public universities headquartered in the surrounding municipalities also have secondary campuses in Madrid proper: it is the case of the [Charles III University of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_III_University_of_Madrid) (*Universidad Carlos III de Madrid*, UC3M) with its main site in [Getafe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Getafe) and an educational facility in [Embajadores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embajadores).[^cite_note-255] The [King Juan Carlos University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Juan_Carlos_University) (*Universidad Rey Juan Carlos*, URJC) has its main site in [Móstoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%B3stoles) and a secondary campus in [Vicálvaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vic%C3%A1lvaro). The private [Camilo José Cela University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universidad_Camilo_Jos%C3%A9_Cela) (*Universidad Camilo José Cela*, UCJC) has a postgraduate school in [Chamberí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber%C3%AD).&#x20;

### Business schools



Students of the IE Business School

[IE Business School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IE_Business_School) (formerly Instituto de Empresa) has its main campus on the border of the Chamartín and Salamanca districts of Madrid. IE Business School recently ranked #1 in WSJ's 2009 rankings for Best MBA Programs under 2 years. It scored ahead of usual stalwarts, [INSEAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INSEAD) and [IMD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Management_Development), giving it top billing among International MBA programs. Although based in [Barcelona](4443.md), both [IESE Business School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IESE) and [ESADE Business School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESADE_Business_School) also have Madrid campuses.[^cite_note-Sánchez-Silva-256]&#x20;

These three schools are the top-ranked [business schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_school) in Spain, consistently rank among the top 20 business schools globally, and offer [MBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master_of_Business_Administration) programs, in English or Spanish, as well as other business degrees. Madrid is a good destination for business schools and a city much desired by foreign students. The most important Spanish business schools (IESE, IE, ESADE) have invested 125 million euros in expanding their campuses in Madrid in 2020.[^cite_note-Sánchez-Silva-256]&#x20;

Other Madrid business schools and universities that have MBA programs include: [EAE Business School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EAE_Business_School) (in English and Spanish), the [Charles III University of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_III_University_of_Madrid) through the *Centro de Ampliación de Estudios* (in English or Spanish); the [Comillas Pontifical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comillas_Pontifical_University) (in Spanish only) and the [Technical University of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technical_University_of_Madrid) (in Spanish only).&#x20;

## Transport

Main article: [Transport in Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Madrid)

In 2018, Madrid banned all non-resident vehicles from its downtown areas.[^cite_note-Jones-2018-257][^cite_note-258]&#x20;



The M-607 meets the M-30 north of the municipality.

Madrid is served by several roads and three modes of public surface transport, and two airports, one of them being almost two different airports. A great many important road, rail and air links converge on the capital, providing effective connections with other parts of the metropolitan region and with the rest of Spain and other parts of Europe.&#x20;

In the 1960s, sweeping urban reforms were promoted to accommodate Madrid to the private car (most notably the removal of boulevards and the incorporation of overpasses), in some ways similarly to other European cities, but in the distinct context of poverty of public debate, which was limited by a dictatorship putting its own interests and those of its clientelist networks before other concerns when it came to alter the urban fabric, thereby marginizalising the pedestrian.[^cite_note-259]&#x20;

### Road transport

Madrid Central

Main article: [Madrid Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Central)

Cars (except for hybrid and electric vehicles as well as residents and guests) were banned in the [Madrid Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Central) low-emission zone in 2018.[^cite_note-citylabcarban-260][^cite_note-261] Pollution in the area dropped following the ban.[^cite_note-262][^cite_note-citylabcarban-260] In 2016 it was announced that Madrid will stop the use of all diesel powered cars and trucks within the next decade.[^cite_note-263]&#x20;

Radial roads



The network of high capacity roads in Spain features its most important node in Madrid.

Madrid is the centre of the most important roads of Spain. Already in 1720, the *Reglamento General de Postas* enacted by [Philip V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_V_of_Spain) configurated the basis of a radial system of roads in the country.[^cite_note-264]&#x20;

Madrid features a number of the most prominent *[autovías](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autov%C3%ADa)* (fast dualled highways), part of the [State Road Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=State_Road_Network\&action=edit\&redlink=1). Clock-wise starting from the north: the [A-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autov%C3%ADa_A-1) (Madrid–[Irún](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ir%C3%BAn)–French border), [A-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autov%C3%ADa_A-2) (Madrid–[Zaragoza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaragoza)–[Barcelona](4443.md)–French border), [A-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autov%C3%ADa_A-3) (Madrid–[Valencia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valencia,_Spain)), [A-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autov%C3%ADa_A-4) (Madrid–[Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B3rdoba,_Spain)–[Sevilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sevilla)–[Cádiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%A1diz)), [A-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autov%C3%ADa_A-5) (Madrid–[Badajoz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badajoz)–Portuguese border) and the [A-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autov%C3%ADa_A-6) (Madrid–[A Coruña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Coru%C3%B1a)). The [A-42](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autov%C3%ADa_A-42), another highway connecting Madrid to [Toledo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toledo,_Spain), is also part of the State Network.&#x20;

The [M-607](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=M-607\&action=edit\&redlink=1) connects Madrid to the [Puerto de Navacerrada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_de_Navacerrada). It is a fast dualled highway in its initial stretch from Madrid to [Colmenar Viejo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colmenar_Viejo), and part of the [Regional Road Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Regional_Road_Network\&action=edit\&redlink=1) (in relation to the concerning administration, not to the technical features of the road).&#x20;

Due to the large amount of traffic, new toll highways were built parallel to the main national freeways. Their names are [R-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=R-2_motorway_\(Spain\)\&action=edit\&redlink=1), [R-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-3_motorway_\(Spain\)), [R-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-4_motorway_\(Spain\)) and [R-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=R-5_motorway_\(Spain\)\&action=edit\&redlink=1) and they were intended to provide a paid alternative to the often overcrowded free radials. However, except the R-3, they do not end close to the M-30 innermost ring road, as the R-2 finishes in the M-40, the R-4 in the M-50 and the R-5 in the M-40.&#x20;

Orbital roads



M-30 tunnel parallel to the Manzanares

Also Madrid road network includes four [orbital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ring_road) ones at different distances from the centre. The innermost ring-road, the [M-30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autopista_de_Circunvalaci%C3%B3n_M-30), is the only one with its path strictly located within the Madrid municipal limits. It is owned by the [Madrid City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_City_Council) and operated by Madrid Calle 30, S.A. It is the busiest Spanish road, famous for its traffic jams. A significant portion of the southern part runs underground parallel to the [Manzanares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manzanares_River), with tunnel sections of more than 6 km (3.7 mi) in length and 3 to 6 lanes in each direction.&#x20;

The second ring-road, the [M-40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autopista_de_Circunvalaci%C3%B3n_M-40) (part of the State Road Network) circles the city, while also extending to other surrounding municipalities. A NW stretch of the road runs underground, below the southern reaches of the [Monte de El Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monte_de_El_Pardo) protected area.&#x20;

The [M-45](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Autopista_de_Circunvalaci%C3%B3n_M-45\&action=edit\&redlink=1) partially circles the city, connecting the M-40 and M-50, passing through areas like Villaverde and Vallecas in the southeast of the municipality.&#x20;

The [M-50](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autopista_de_Circunvalaci%C3%B3n_M-50), the Madrid's outer ring road, connects municipalities and cities in the metropolitan area, like [Fuenlabrada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuenlabrada), [Móstoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%B3stoles), [Getafe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Getafe), [Leganés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legan%C3%A9s) in the south and [Boadilla del Monte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boadilla_del_Monte) and [Las Rozas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Rozas_de_Madrid) in the west.&#x20;

### Public transport



Map of the [Madrid Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Metro)



[Cercanías Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cercan%C3%ADas_Madrid) map

There are four major components of public transport, with many [intermodal interchanges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_hub). The [Consorcio Regional de Transportes de Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consorcio_Regional_de_Transportes_de_Madrid) (CRTM) coordinates the [public transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transport) operations across multiple providers in the region,[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266] harmonising fares for the commuter rail, rapid transit, light rail and bus transport services provided by different operators.&#x20;

Metro

Main article: [Madrid Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Metro)

The Metro is the [rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) system serving Madrid as well as some suburbs. Founded in 1919, it underwent extensive enlargement in the second half of the 20th century.[^cite_note-transport-267] It is the third longest metro system in Europe (after [Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Metro) and [London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Subway)) at 294 km (183 mi). As of 2019, it has 302 stations.[^cite_note-268] Only the [Paris Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Metro) has more stations. It features 13 lines; 12 of them are colour-coded and numbered 1 to 12 ([Line 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_1_\(Madrid_Metro\)), [Line 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_2_\(Madrid_Metro\)), [Line 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_3_\(Madrid_Metro\)), [Line 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_4_\(Madrid_Metro\)), [Line 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_5_\(Madrid_Metro\)), [Line 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_6_\(Madrid_Metro\)), [Line 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_7_\(Madrid_Metro\)), [Line 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_8_\(Madrid_Metro\)), [Line 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_9_\(Madrid_Metro\)), [Line 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_10_\(Madrid_Metro\)), [Line 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_11_\(Madrid_Metro\)) and [Line 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_12_\(Madrid_Metro\))), while the other one, the short *[Ramal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramal_\(Madrid_Metro\))* (R), links Ópera to Príncipe Pío.[^cite_note-transport-267]&#x20;

Cercanías

Main article: [Cercanías Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cercan%C3%ADas_Madrid)

Cercanías Madrid is the [commuter rail](6286.md) service used for longer distances from the suburbs and beyond into Madrid, consisting of nine lines totalling 578 km (359 mi) and more than 90 stations. With fewer stops inside the centre of the city they are faster than the Metro, but run less frequently. This system is connected with Metro (presently 22 stations) and Light Metro. The lines are named: [C-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-1_\(Cercan%C3%ADas_Madrid\)), [C-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-2_\(Cercan%C3%ADas_Madrid\)), [C-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-3_\(Cercan%C3%ADas_Madrid\)), [C-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-4_\(Cercan%C3%ADas_Madrid\)), [C-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-5_\(Cercan%C3%ADas_Madrid\)), [C-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-7_\(Cercan%C3%ADas_Madrid\)), [C-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-8_\(Cercan%C3%ADas_Madrid\)), [C-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-9_\(Cercan%C3%ADas_Madrid\)), [C-10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-10_\(Cercan%C3%ADas_Madrid\)), respectively.&#x20;

Buses

There is a dense network of [bus](4146.md) routes, run by the municipal company [Empresa Municipal de Transportes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empresa_Municipal_de_Transportes_de_Madrid) (or EMT Madrid), which operates 24 hours a day; special services called "N lines" are run during nighttime. The special Airport Express Shuttle line connecting the airport with the city centre features distinctively yellow buses. In addition to the urban lines operated by the EMT, the green buses (*interurbanos*) connect the city with the suburbs. The later lines, while also regulated by the CRTM, are often run by private operators.&#x20;

Almost half of all journeys in the metropolitan area are made on public transport, a very high proportion compared with most European cities.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 62–4 </sup> Madrid has 15723 taxis around all the city.&#x20;

Taxi

The taxicabs are regulated by a specific sub-division of taxi service, a body dependent of the [Madrid City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_City_Council). The authorisation entails a badge for the vehicle and a license for the driver, who has to be older than 18.[^cite_note-269] Since the 1970s, the fleet of taxis has remained stable roughly around 16,000 vehicles, accounting for 15,600 in 2014.[^cite_note-270]&#x20;

### Long-distance transport



[AVE](523382.md) rolling stock at the [Madrid Atocha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Atocha) station

In terms of longer-distance transport, Madrid is the central node of the system of *[autovías](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autov%C3%ADa)*, giving the city direct fast road links with most parts of Spain and with France and Portugal. It is also the focal point of [Spain's high-speed rail system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail_in_Spain). The most demanded train routes link Madrid to [Barcelona](4443.md), [Valencia](63861.md), [Seville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seville), and [Málaga](184763.md).[^cite_note-271] There are now 2,900 km (1,800 mi) of high-speed rail track, connecting Madrid with 17 provincial capitals, and further lines are under construction.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 72–75 </sup>&#x20;

Spanish companies are designing new high-speed trains which will be the new-generation [AVE](523382.md), much like the current [Talgo AVRIL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talgo_AVRIL).&#x20;

Aside from the local and regional bus commuting services, Madrid is also a node for long-distance bus connections to many national destinations. The [Estación Sur de Autobuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Estaci%C3%B3n_Sur_de_Autobuses\&action=edit\&redlink=1) in Méndez Álvaro, the busiest bus station in the country,[^cite_note-ep-272] also features international bus connections to cities in Morocco as well as to diverse European destinations.[^cite_note-ep-272]&#x20;

### Airport

Main article: [Madrid-Barajas Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid-Barajas_Airport)



Interior of the terminal 4 (T4) of the [Madrid–Barajas Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid%E2%80%93Barajas_Airport)

Madrid is also home to the Madrid-Barajas Airport, the sixth-busiest airport in Europe, handling over 60 million passengers annually, of whom 70% are international travellers, in addition to the majority of Spain's [air freight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_freight) movements.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 76–78 </sup> Barajas is a major European hub, largely westward facing, specialised in the Americas, with a comparatively lighter connectivity to Asia.[^cite_note-273] Madrid's location at the centre of the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) makes it a major [logistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logistics) base.[^cite_note-Estructura-142]<sup>: 79–80 </sup> Madrid-Barajas Airport has 4 terminals plus terminal 4S, called the Satellite terminal; this terminal is 2 km (1.2 mi) from terminal 4 and connected by an [automated people mover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automated_people_mover).&#x20;

The smaller (and older) [Cuatro Vientos Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuatro_Vientos_Airport) has a dual military-civilian use and hosts several aviation schools. The [Torrejón Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torrej%C3%B3n_Air_Base), located in the neighbouring municipality of [Torrejón de Ardoz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torrej%C3%B3n_de_Ardoz), also has a secondary civilian use aside from the military purpose.&#x20;

## International relations

### Diplomacy

Madrid hosts [121 foreign embassies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_missions_in_Spain) accredited before Spain,[^cite_note-274] comprising the totality of resident embassies in the country. The headquarters of the Spanish [Ministry of Foreign Affairs, European Union and Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Spain\)), the [Spanish Agency for International Development Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Agency_for_International_Development_Cooperation) and the [Diplomatic School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic_School_of_Spain) are also located in the city.&#x20;

### International organisations

Madrid hosts the seat of international organisations such as the United Nations' [World Tourism Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Tourism_Organization) (UNWTO), the [Ibero-American General Secretariat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibero-American_General_Secretariat) (SEGIB), the [Organization of Ibero-American States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Ibero-American_States) (OEI), the [International Youth Organism for Iberoamerica](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=International_Youth_Organism_for_Iberoamerica\&action=edit\&redlink=1) (OIJ), the [Ibero-American Organization of Social Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ibero-American_Organization_of_Social_Security\&action=edit\&redlink=1) (OISS), the [International Organization of Securities Commissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Organization_of_Securities_Commissions) (IOSCO), the [Club of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Club_of_Madrid) and the [International Commission for the Conservation of Atlantic Tunas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Commission_for_the_Conservation_of_Atlantic_Tunas) (ICCAT).&#x20;

### Union of Ibero-American Capital Cities

Madrid is part of the [Union of Ibero-American Capital Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Ibero-American_Capital_Cities)[^cite_note-277] establishing brotherly relations with the following cities through the issuing of a collective statement in October 1982:&#x20;

- [Asunción](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asunci%C3%B3n), Paraguay

- [Bogotá](211271.md), Colombia

[ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [8424916255](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/8424916255). Se produce aspiración en Andalucía, Extremadura, Canarias, Murcia, Toledo, en la Mancha, en ciertas partes de las provincias de Madrid (en la capital está muy extendida hasta en las clases cultas), Cuenca, Ávila, Salamanca, Rioja Baja.

[^cite_note-Gili-197]: &#x20;Citado en Quilis, *Los Pronombres "le", "la", "lo" y sus plurales en la lengua española hablada en Madrid&#x20;*, pag. 36. \
    &#x20;*Según Gili Gaya (1958, 210–211), el 'le' dativo, que, como se sabe, es invariable para los 2 géneros, se ha especializado como masculino: 'le regalaron una bicicleta' (a él); y cuando es femeninose sustituye por 'la', procedente del acusativo: la regalaron una bicicleta' (a ella). "Este es el uso madrileño espontáneo en todas las clases sociales, a no ser entre personas cuya instrucción gramatical, o la procedencia de otras regiones, lo corrija más o menos. La influencia de la capital irradia su laísmo hacia otras provincias del Centro y del Norte, llegando a vencer a menudo la resistencia del lenguaje literario. El vulgo madrileño va todavía más allá: el 'lo' sustituye con frecuencia a 'le' como dativo: 'lo pegaron una bofetada'. Sin embargo, este 'loísmo' se siente en todas partes como extremadamente plebeyo, y no ha logrado salir del habla achulapada."*
